                                           PART V

                        ANTI-PATTERNS
This Part reverses the lens. It exposes recurring architecture shapes that hide authority, state,
    effects, security, reliability, or operating cost, and pairs each failure with a measurable
                                         replacement pattern.




                                      BOOK v1.0.0   |   PAGE 89
                                                             ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE




17 — Enterprise Anti-Pattern Catalog
Field-guide method
Anti-patterns are recurring system shapes. Diagnose the failed responsibility boundary, not only the symptom. Each card
supplies the same replacement sequence.




                         Figure 17-1 — Autonomous monolith versus governed agentic architecture


Reasoning
AP-01 — Autonomous monolith
Problem. A prototype mixes reasoning, state, credentials, tools, retries, approval, completion.
Anti-Pattern. One unconstrained agent owns end-to-end control.
Why It Fails. Probabilistic behavior becomes the control plane and failures cannot be isolated.
Recommended Pattern. Bounded runtime inside durable workflow, policy, tool/effect, acceptance services.
Architecture. Monolith → direct tools → “done” versus workflow → semantic nodes → governed effects → acceptance.
Enterprise Example. Onboarding restarts and repeats vendor creation.
Detection Metrics. Direct protected calls, restart loss, false completion, mixed responsibilities.
Trade-offs. Decompose around highest-risk effects; more services add interfaces.

Context engineering
AP-02 — Prompt as database
Problem. Business state must survive.
Anti-Pattern. Chat history or summary is authoritative.
Why It Fails. Truncation, drift, concurrency, schema, and audit fail.
Recommended Pattern. Canonical typed state plus ContextManifest projection.



                                                  BOOK v1.0.0   |   PAGE 90
                                                             ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



Architecture. Transcript state versus State Service → snapshot → prompt.
Enterprise Example. Renewal forgets legal approval after summarization.
Detection Metrics. Compaction loss, summary conflict, recovery mismatch.
Trade-offs. Typed state needs schema evolution.

Security
AP-03 — Ambient authority
Problem. Agents need access.
Anti-Pattern. Workers receive reusable broad credentials.
Why It Fails. Compromise obtains broad, long-lived authority.
Recommended Pattern. Subject+workload identity and narrowed capability leases.
Architecture. Broad token versus policy → scoped lease → enforcement.
Enterprise Example. Support token can export all customers.
Detection Metrics. Scope/age, unused privilege, expansion, cross-tenant attempts.
Trade-offs. Fine policy adds design/latency.

Reliability
AP-04 — Retry everywhere
Problem. Transient failures occur.
Anti-Pattern. SDK, gateway, workflow, connector, tool each retry.
Why It Fails. Attempts multiply, cost/load spike, mutations duplicate.
Recommended Pattern. One retry owner per failure class and shared attempt/deadline budget.
Architecture. Nested retries versus classified failure → one schedule → terminal.
Enterprise Example. 3×3×2 policies create 18 registry calls.
Detection Metrics. Attempts/logical operation, amplification, duplicates, deadline overrun.
Trade-offs. Central policy can be conservative.

Reliability
AP-05 — Timeout means failure
Problem. Response is lost after dispatch.
Anti-Pattern. Mark failed and retry immediately.
Why It Fails. First call may have committed.
Recommended Pattern. Unknown-effect terminal and reconciliation before retry/compensate.
Architecture. Timeout→failed→retry versus timeout→unknown→lookup→decide.
Enterprise Example. Vendor is created twice.
Detection Metrics. Unknown count/age, duplicate keys, post-timeout conflicts.
Trade-offs. Unknown may delay completion.

Governance
AP-06 — Boolean or conversational approval
Problem. Humans approve protected actions.
Anti-Pattern. A Boolean/chat message authorizes later current arguments.

                                                   BOOK v1.0.0   |   PAGE 91
                                                               ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



Why It Fails. Effect, policy, actor, or version can change.
Recommended Pattern. Immutable proposal and expiring exact ApprovalGrant.
Architecture. approved=true versus proposal hash → grant → revalidate → commit.
Enterprise Example. $10k approval reused for $100k.
Detection Metrics. Hash mismatch, reuse, expiry, post-approval incident.
Trade-offs. Benign changes may require reapproval.

Observability
AP-07 — Model says done
Problem. Runs need completion.
Anti-Pattern. Model assertion sets success.
Why It Fails. Missing evidence, approval, effect, or obligation hides as success.
Recommended Pattern. Independent executable acceptance over authoritative state.
Architecture. Model text→success versus AcceptanceEngine→accepted/blocked/failed/unknown.
Enterprise Example. Onboarding says complete with stale sanctions check.
Detection Metrics. False completion, reopen rate, missing artifacts/effects.
Trade-offs. Rules require domain maintenance.

Multi-agent collaboration
AP-08 — Multi-agent chat as workflow
Problem. Specialists coordinate.
Anti-Pattern. Free-form messages own state, work, deadlines, joins.
Why It Fails. Duplicate work, cycles, lost cancellation/artifacts.
Recommended Pattern. TaskEnvelope, owner, child tasks, artifacts, deterministic join.
Architecture. Chat mesh versus supervisor workflow + task service.
Enterprise Example. Finance and legal each expect the other to approve.
Detection Metrics. Orphans, conflicts, depth, join age, late effects.
Trade-offs. Formal tasks reduce fluidity.

Context engineering
AP-09 — Vector store as source of truth
Problem. Relevant knowledge is needed.
Anti-Pattern. Similarity hit is current authoritative fact.
Why It Fails. Stale, unauthorized, poisoned, duplicated chunks drive action.
Recommended Pattern. Governed source/snapshot; index is derived locator with ACL/freshness verification.
Architecture. Vector hit→action versus source+ACL+snapshot→evidence→decision.
Enterprise Example. Old policy chunk applies obsolete limit.
Detection Metrics. Freshness, source disagreement, ACL/citation mismatch.
Trade-offs. Authoritative lookup may be slower.




                                                     BOOK v1.0.0   |   PAGE 92
                                                               ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE




Security
AP-10 — LLM judge as security gate
Problem. Ambiguous content needs review.
Anti-Pattern. Model score alone authorizes protected action.
Why It Fails. Judge is injectable, nondeterministic, biased, and drifts.
Recommended Pattern. Deterministic policy/invariant gate; judge only triages/evidences.
Architecture. score>threshold→execute versus policy→optional judge→human/escalation.
Enterprise Example. Judge calls excessive payment low risk.
Detection Metrics. Repeated-run disagreement, bypass, false negative, override.
Trade-offs. Rules cannot express all nuance.

Reasoning
AP-11 — Unbounded loops and recursive delegation
Problem. Complex work varies.
Anti-Pattern. Agents iterate/spawn without global limits.
Why It Fails. Runaway cost, cyclic delegation, queue saturation.
Recommended Pattern. Global decreasing budgets, acyclic ownership, depth/fan-out/progress limits.
Architecture. Recursion versus runtime-governed task graph.
Enterprise Example. Research supervisor spawns endless fact-checkers.
Detection Metrics. Depth, fan-out, repeated state, no progress, budget exhaustion.
Trade-offs. Caps may truncate rare work.

Tools
AP-12 — Ungated tool discovery and catalog dumping
Problem. Many tools exist.
Anti-Pattern. Every discovered tool/schema enters the prompt.
Why It Fails. Confusion, token load, malicious descriptions, excessive agency.
Recommended Pattern. Registry → eligibility/policy → task shortlist → schema validation.
Architecture. Full MCP catalog versus approved tool slice and lease.
Enterprise Example. Reporting agent finds admin delete tool.
Detection Metrics. Exposed/eligible/used, confusion, overhead, unauthorized discovery.
Trade-offs. Narrow list can omit needed tool.

Memory
AP-13 — Memory write by default
Problem. Personalization is desired.
Anti-Pattern. Every interaction/summary becomes durable memory.
Why It Fails. Secrets, errors, injections, bias, stale facts persist.
Recommended Pattern. Purpose-bound candidate with provenance, verification, scope, TTL, deletion.
Architecture. Transcript→memory versus candidate→gate→typed record.
Enterprise Example. Malicious email stores fake bank account.


                                                    BOOK v1.0.0   |   PAGE 93
                                                              ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



Detection Metrics. Sensitive writes, stale retrieval, corrections, memory incidents.
Trade-offs. Strict writes reduce convenience.

Observability
AP-14 — Telemetry without causal/effect lineage
Problem. Teams instrument components.
Anti-Pattern. Logs cannot join versions, policy, effects, acceptance, outcome.
Why It Fails. Local optimization hides business regression.
Recommended Pattern. Causal run graph joining release, context, decisions, effects, outcome/cost.
Architecture. Separate dashboards versus correlated graph.
Enterprise Example. Lower model latency hides more retries and lower completion.
Detection Metrics. Outcome join, missing parents/versions, unattributed cost.
Trade-offs. Correlation increases governance burden.

System architecture
AP-15 — Model/provider swap as configuration
Problem. Teams seek cost/quality changes.
Anti-Pattern. Alias changes without behavioral validation.
Why It Fails. Tool, schema, refusal, safety, latency, cost behavior shifts.
Recommended Pattern. Versioned ChangeBundle with evaluation, security, load, canary, rollback.
Architecture. Edit alias versus immutable route policy→replay→approval→canary.
Enterprise Example. Cheaper model drops tool args in one language.
Detection Metrics. Schema/tool regression, refusal shift, slice drift, fallback.
Trade-offs. Validation slows experimentation.

Operations
AP-16 — In-place change and test after release
Problem. Fast edits are convenient.
Anti-Pattern. Prompts/Skills/tools/policy/routes mutate production.
Why It Fails. Runs are irreproducible; rollback/attribution fail.
Recommended Pattern. Immutable artifacts, ReleaseManifest, pretests, signed promotion, canary, rollback.
Architecture. Mutable console versus build→validate→approve→promote→monitor.
Enterprise Example. Prompt fix increases leakage and cannot roll back exactly.
Detection Metrics. Unversioned runs, out-of-band edits, rollback failure.
Trade-offs. Immutability needs good tooling.

Transition to Chapter 18
After removing unsafe local architectures, Chapter 18 considers when multiple agents add justified specialization rather
than accidental complexity.

                                         PART VI

        MULTI-AGENT & PLATFORM
              INTEGRATION
This Part expands beyond one runtime. It shows when specialized agents are justified and how
MCP, A2A, APIs, events, and shared services can cross platform boundaries without transferring
                                 hidden authority or trust.

18 — Supervisor/Worker and Multi-Agent
Collaboration
     Evidence note. Public multi-agent implementation reports describe supervisor/specialist designs and substantial
     token/context trade-offs; these observations justify explicit topology and cost tests rather than universal multi-agent
     adoption. [R28][R33]

  NORTHSTAR AT THIS POINT Screening, tax, security, and procurement specialists can run concurrently, but a
  plausible multi-agent conversation is not a coordination protocol. Northstar defines durable tasks, one owner per
  task, narrowed authority, explicit joins, and artifact contracts.



  DESIGN DECISION Give one component ownership of each task, derive narrower child authority, and define explicit
  join and cancellation semantics.




What this chapter delivers
Give each task one owner, narrow authority on delegation, and make fan-out, fan-in, cancellation, and conflicts explicit.

Solution blueprint
Figure 12. Supervisor/Worker and Multi-Agent Collaboration.
State owner                   Trust boundary               Scale unit                      Failure containment         Core contracts

Task Service and supervisor   parent/child authority and                                                               TaskEnvelope · ChildGrant ·
                                                           task tree · specialist queue    one child task
workflow                      artifact adoption                                                                        JoinPolicy




Collaboration is a state machine
A supervisor/worker topology is justified when specialists have different tools, data boundaries, owners, SLOs, or
deployment needs. Northstar’s supervisor owns the onboarding outcome and creates durable child tasks for screening,
tax, and security. Each child receives only the capability, evidence, budget, deadline, and cancellation scope it needs.
Fan-in is explicit: wait for all, quorum, first valid, or fallback. Artifact conflicts use deterministic precedence, source
freshness, confidence thresholds, or human resolution. Free-form conversation may help agents explain results, but it
cannot replace task status, ownership, artifact, or effect records.
Nested delegation multiplies cost and risk. Northstar caps depth, children, concurrency, total turns, total tokens, external
calls, and wall-clock time across the entire task tree. Parent cancellation propagates as durable intent; a child with an
executing mutation must reconcile before acknowledging final cancellation.

Agent topology, orchestration, delegation, and termination

                                                                            Divide work without creating ambiguous ownership, recursive loops,

                                                                            Task boundaries are semantic; model routing is fallible; every new

Recommended orchestration rules
    Creation: instantiate agents from an immutable AgentManifest; never construct production instructions and
     permissions ad hoc in a prompt.
    Delegation: create a child task with explicit goal, input artifact references, output schema, authority ceiling, budget,
     deadline, trace context, and parent run ID.
    Handoff: atomically transfer the conversation owner and issue a new capability envelope; preserve the prior agent
     as history, not as a competing owner.
    Coordination: use a workflow node, not a group chat, to decide readiness, joins, retries, cancellation, and
     aggregation.
    Shared state: store it in a concurrency-controlled state service. Agents receive snapshots and return proposed
     patches with expected versions.
    Termination: terminal states are deterministic—acceptance criteria satisfied, user canceled, policy denied,
     budget/time/step limit reached, or unrecoverable error. The model may recommend completion but cannot invent the
     terminal transition.
    Plan changes: append a versioned plan revision with rationale and diff; validate new nodes against the original
     authority envelope. Materially broader scope requires new consent or approval.

                                                                       ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE

State, consistency, and scale
      Partition operational rows, workflow queues, and read models by tenant\_id + run\_id; all tasks in one run should
       have a stable home shard so parent/child transitions are local.
      Let the durable workflow instance serialize control decisions for one run. Scale across runs horizontally; do not use a
       global coordinator or distributed lock for ordinary task ownership.
      Dispatch by capability + region + risk\_class + priority. A receiver queue contains task references, not full sensitive
       context; workers fetch authorized artifacts after assignment.
      Use a strongly consistent transaction for task creation, owner transfer, terminal transition, and budget reservation.
       Graph/UI projections, search indexes, and analytics may be eventually consistent.
      Enforce manifest-level defaults such as max\_delegation\_depth, max\_children\_per\_task, max\_parallel\_children,
       and max\_plan\_revisions; risk policy may lower them per run.
      Store the authoritative graph as normalized tasks/edges; publish an incrementally updated materialized projection
       for fast operator queries. Large evidence and messages remain in object storage.
      Mitigate “hot runs” by batching status events, limiting concurrent ready nodes, and applying per-run semaphores. A
       single run should not require more coordinator throughput than its bounded fan-out permits.
      Idempotent task creation and completion make queue redelivery safe. Task terminal state plus accepted artifact
       hash is immutable; corrections create a new revision/task rather than reopening history.

                                           capability, ownership, trust, isolation, or

                                           parallelism justifies coordination cost, and
                                           evaluate against the strongest single-agent
                                           baseline.

Pattern reference — Supervisor/Worker
    PROBLEM One outcome requires specialist work that benefits from parallelism or separate capability boundaries
    while a single component retains overall ownership.

Architecture and flow
The supervisor creates typed child tasks with narrower grants and acceptance criteria. Workers return artifacts; the
supervisor or deterministic join validates and aggregates them.
194.       Decompose only along meaningful specialist boundaries.
195.       Create durable TaskEnvelopes and child capability envelopes.
196.       Schedule independently with deadlines and cancellation lineage.
197.       Collect artifacts under explicit join semantics.
198.       Resolve conflicts and accept the parent outcome.

Pattern reference — Multi-Agent Collaboration
    PROBLEM Several autonomous or semi-autonomous components must exchange tasks and artifacts while
    preserving ownership, authority, evidence, and recovery.



Architecture and flow
Collaboration uses durable TaskEnvelope, message, status, artifact, cancellation, and join contracts; conversation is
advisory, while workflow state determines truth.
199.       Create a task with owner, goal, acceptance, grant, budget, and deadline.
200.       Exchange authenticated versioned messages and artifacts.
201.       Persist statuses and correlation independently of chat context.
202.       Adopt artifacts only after schema, provenance, quality, and policy validation.
203.       Join, cancel, escalate, or compensate under explicit rules.

Monotonic child authority
PYTHON · implementation excerpt
    PYTHON

1            from __future__ import annotations
2
3            from dataclasses import dataclass



                                                       BOOK v1.0.0      |   PAGE 101
                                                           ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



4      from datetime import datetime, timezone
5
6
7      def _resource_within(child: str, parent: str) -> bool:
8          if parent.endswith("://"):
9              return child.startswith(parent)
10         normalized = parent.rstrip("/")
11         return child == normalized or child.startswith(normalized + "/")
12
13
14     @dataclass(frozen=True)
15     class CapabilityEnvelope:
16         grant_id: str
17         subject_id: str
18         actor_id: str
19         actions: frozenset[str]
20         resource_prefixes: frozenset[str]
21         purposes: frozenset[str]
22         data_classes: frozenset[str]
23         regions: frozenset[str]
24         maximum_risk: int
25         maximum_cost_usd: float
26         delegation_depth: int
27         expires_at: datetime
28
29         def assert_current(self, now: datetime | None = None) -> None:
30             current = now or datetime.now(timezone.utc)
31             if self.expires_at <= current:
32                 raise PermissionError("capability envelope expired")
33
34
35     def derive_child(
36         parent: CapabilityEnvelope,
37         child: CapabilityEnvelope,
38         *,
39         now: datetime | None = None,
40     ) -> CapabilityEnvelope:
41         """Prove monotonic narrowing; actor identity may change, subject may not."""
42
43         parent.assert_current(now)
44         if child.subject_id != parent.subject_id:
45             raise PermissionError("subject cannot change")
46         if not child.actions <= parent.actions:
47             raise PermissionError("actions broadened")
48         if not child.purposes <= parent.purposes:
49             raise PermissionError("purposes broadened")
50         if not child.data_classes <= parent.data_classes:
51             raise PermissionError("data classes broadened")
52         if not child.regions <= parent.regions:
53             raise PermissionError("regions broadened")
54         if child.maximum_risk > parent.maximum_risk:
55             raise PermissionError("risk ceiling raised")
56         if child.maximum_cost_usd > parent.maximum_cost_usd:
57             raise PermissionError("cost ceiling raised")
58         if child.delegation_depth >= parent.delegation_depth:
59             raise PermissionError("delegation depth was not reduced")
60         if child.expires_at > parent.expires_at:
61             raise PermissionError("lifetime extended")
62         for resource in child.resource_prefixes:
63             if not any(_resource_within(resource, allowed) for allowed in parent.resource_prefixes):
64                 raise PermissionError("resource scope broadened")
65         return child




Debugging playbook
204.   Build the durable task tree and identify the current owner of every node.
205.   Verify each child grant is a subset of the parent grant.
206.   Inspect join semantics, missing artifacts, duplicates, and conflicts.




                                                 BOOK v1.0.0   |   PAGE 102
                                                                     ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE




Production checklist
✓ Another agent exists only when expertise, tools, data, trust, ownership, SLO, or scaling truly differs.
✓ Delegation creates a typed durable task with one current owner.
✓ Child authority is provably narrower than parent authority.
✓ Joins define all, quorum, first-valid, or fallback semantics and conflict policy.
✓ MCP and A2A pass through gateways that verify peers, schemas, artifacts, quotas, and cancellation.

Exercises
207.       Design supervisor/worker fan-out with quorum and a deterministic conflict rule.
208.       Model cascading cancellation across two local agents and one remote task.

Technical review questions
Question                                                                 Strong answer

                                                                         When meaningful boundaries differ—domain, tools, data access,
When is a multi-agent system justified?                                  owner, deployment, SLO, or cost—not merely because multiple
                                                                         personas sound sophisticated.

                                                                         An in-run specialist with parent control; conversational control
Compare an agent-as-tool, a handoff, and a durable child task.           transfer; or independently owned durable work with retries, waits,
                                                                         deadlines, and cancellation.

                                                                         Derive child authority by intersection; narrow action, resource, data,
How do you prevent delegation from escalating privilege?                 time, budget, and depth; reauthorize each invocation; issue scoped
                                                                         last-hop credentials.

                                                                         Persist child states and artifacts, wait for an explicit join condition,
How should fan-in be implemented?                                        then validate schemas, provenance, conflicts, quality, and policy
                                                                         deterministically.

                                                                         Business authorization, peer trust, quality, tenant isolation, effect
What does an A2A or MCP protocol not solve?                              semantics, artifact safety, cancellation guarantees, and audit remain
                                                                         local platform responsibilities.

Multi-agent collaboration inside one platform is only part of the problem; Chapter 19 governs cross-platform tools,
agents, APIs, and events.

19 — MCP, A2A, and External Integration
    Evidence note. MCP 2026-07-28 defines a stateless, self-contained protocol core for resources, prompts, and
    tools, while A2A v1.0.1 defines agent cards and task/message/artifact exchange. Neither protocol transfers
    application authorization or business acceptance automatically. [R05][R06][R07]

  NORTHSTAR AT THIS POINT Northstar consumes a third-party screening MCP server and delegates regional
  verification to a partner agent through A2A. Protocol conformance makes messages interoperable; it does not make
  the endpoint trusted or the artifact safe to adopt.



  DESIGN DECISION Terminate MCP and A2A at governed local gateways; verify endpoints, artifacts, authority,
  quotas, and effect semantics locally.




What this chapter delivers
Federate tools and agents through locally governed gateways, registries, task protocols, and artifact adoption controls.

Solution blueprint
Figure 13. MCP, A2A, and External Integration.
State owner              Trust boundary                   Scale unit                       Failure containment       Core contracts

                         local trust domain to external                                                              ServerManifest · A2ATask ·
MCP and A2A Gateways                                      endpoint · peer · tenant quota   one remote call or task
                         endpoint or peer                                                                            ArtifactEnvelope




Two protocols, two responsibilities
MCP standardizes how a host discovers and invokes tools, resources, and prompts exposed by a server. A2A
standardizes collaboration around tasks, messages, status, streaming, artifacts, and cancellation. They can coexist: a
local agent may use MCP for a capability and A2A to delegate independently owned work.
Northstar terminates both protocols at gateways. The MCP Gateway resolves a pinned server manifest, authenticates
the endpoint, filters tools, validates schemas, applies tenant and user policy, constrains egress, and wraps mutating calls
in effect semantics. The A2A Gateway verifies peer identity and trust profile, translates local TaskEnvelope to the
negotiated protocol, persists status, enforces deadlines and quotas, and scans returned artifacts before local adoption.

Registry model
A server or peer registry entry contains owner, endpoint, protocol version and profile, authentication method, trust
material, schemas, supported capabilities, data classifications, regions, risk, rate limits, health, release aliases,
evaluation evidence, and revocation state. Discovery never substitutes for authorization.

MLflow MCP Registry note
MLflow 3.15.0 introduced experimental MCP Registry capabilities for registering servers, managing versions and aliases,
creating access endpoints, and discovering tools. Northstar can use this as an operational registry surface while
retaining business authorization, tenant policy, credential exchange, and effect control in its platform gateways. Because
the feature is experimental, pin exact MLflow and registry schema versions and test migration and rollback.

A2A’s task/message/artifact model and async operations are useful at remote-agent boundaries. Its agent card is
discovery metadata—not proof of trust. Pin the card version or digest, verify issuer and endpoint, and filter advertised
skills through enterprise policy.

Ownership and failure rules
     A task has exactly one current owner and one parent coordinator; leases may move execution, but ownership
      changes are transactional and audited.
     The receiver may reject, accept, request input, produce partial artifacts, complete, fail, or acknowledge cancellation.
      Terminal tasks cannot accept new work; create a new task for follow-up.
     Cancellation propagates downward by default; compensation does not automatically propagate and must follow
      domain policy.
     Child failure is data. The parent chooses fail-fast, quorum, fallback, partial result, targeted retry, or human escalation
      based on the join contract.
     Aggregators validate schema, provenance, duplicate claims, evidence quality, and conflict policy. They do not
      concatenate prose and call it consensus.
     Shared mutable state is prohibited across trust domains. Exchange immutable artifacts or versioned patches; use
      optimistic concurrency for authorized updates.

Detailed design — APIs, data model, and scalability
Component boundaries
The Agent Directory stores validated cards and endpoint metadata; the Peer Trust Registry stores organizational trust
and allowed purposes; the A2A Gateway terminates authentication and protocol bindings; the Task Bridge maps remote
tasks to internal child workflows; the Artifact Broker exchanges immutable references; the Subscription Dispatcher
handles push/status delivery; and the Trace Bridge links remote and local causal graphs.

High-level APIs

                                                                  Register remote agent issuer/domain, trust material, approved

                                                                  Resolve policy-eligible local or remote agents; discovery never

                                                                  returns a grant

At an open boundary, expose the standard A2A agent-card and task/message operations. Internally, normalize every
binding to the platform’s task state, identity, artifact, error, and audit contracts.

Scalability, federation, and failure isolation
   Partition task-relay state by tenant + peer domain + task ID. Serialize updates for one federated task while scaling
    across tasks and peers.
   Prefer asynchronous callbacks or streaming for progress; poll only as a bounded fallback with exponential backoff.
    Persist callbacks before acknowledgment.
   Enforce per-peer and per-skill rate, concurrency, byte-egress, and cost budgets. Circuit-break a misbehaving peer
    without disabling unrelated local agents.
   Exchange large or sensitive data by short-lived, audience-bound artifact references. Validate recipient, region,
    classification, and purpose before issuing each reference.
   Carry W3C trace context when supported and create span links when the remote system returns a different trace.
    Never rely on a remote trace as the enterprise audit record.
   Keep peer card/configuration reads cached by signed digest, but re-evaluate authorization and trust policy per
    task/message. Card changes cannot alter an in-flight task’s pinned contract.
   Bound remote fan-out at the parent. A peer may not recursively delegate unless the task grant explicitly permits a
    depth and the resulting chain remains visible.
   Treat missed heartbeat, callback failure, cancellation ambiguity, and artifact validation failure as distinct states; do
    not automatically resubmit a potentially active remote task.

Debugging playbook
209.       Verify the registry version, endpoint identity, protocol profile, and trust material.
210.       Inspect local authorization and quota decisions for one remote call.
211.       Validate the returned artifact hash, schema, classification, provenance, and scan result.

Production checklist
✓ MCP and A2A endpoints terminate at authenticated governed gateways.
✓ Registry entries pin endpoints, owners, trust, schemas, and versions.
✓ Per-call or per-task authority, quotas, classification, and egress are checked locally.
✓ Remote artifacts are scanned and adopted under local policy.
✓ Cancellation, retries, idempotency, effects, and audit have local semantics.

Exercises
212.       Wrap an MCP tool with a local Tool Gateway policy.
213.       Define an A2A TaskEnvelope with status, cancellation, and artifact adoption semantics.

20 — LLM Gateway and Model Control Plane
Position and boundaries
The LLM Gateway is the controlled boundary between enterprise workloads and model providers or self-hosted
inference. It normalizes model access and enforces model-specific routing, policy, privacy, economics, observability, and
release governance. It does not own business workflow, tool effects, agent delegation, or the external API perimeter.

                                                                   cancellation, compensation

Policy-first routing




                                     Figure 20-2 — Policy-first model-routing decision tree
Filter by tenant, purpose, data class, residency, risk, required capabilities, context length, tool/JSON behavior, and
approved version. Only then optimize expected accepted-outcome quality under latency and cost budgets. Health and
quota further constrain selection. Fallbacks obey the same invariants; “any available model” is not a safe fallback.
Record candidate routes, exclusions/reasons, selected provider/model/version/region, policy digest, budgets, fallback
chain, and experiment cohort. Cache keys must include every security and semantic dependency. Never cache a
protected decision that depends on current authorization or resource version without preserving that dependency.




                                                    BOOK v1.0.0    |   PAGE 113
                                                             ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE




Gateway metrics and trade-offs
Measure route distribution and eligibility, task success/quality by route, schema/tool correctness, refusal/safety,
p50/p95/p99 latency, queue/retry amplification, cache hits and unsafe-key rejection, tokens/cost per accepted outcome,
policy denials, PII/secrets detections, provider error/fallback, alias/version drift, and trace completeness. Centralization
adds latency and blast-radius risk; deploy regionally, isolate tenants/risk classes, use bulkheads, and retain platform-
owned contracts even when buying a gateway.

The gateway controls model access, but not the entire attack surface; Chapter 21 introduces defense in depth across all
agentic trust boundaries.

21 — Agentic AI Security and Guardrails
Architecture
    Evidence note. The threat catalog is cross-checked against OWASP agentic-security guidance, while the trust-
    boundary model follows zero-trust principles that require explicit policy decisions around protected resources.
    [R11][R15][R16]

Defense in depth
Agentic systems combine untrusted language, privileged tools, dynamic context, durable memory, third-party protocols,
and autonomous timing. Security cannot be one prompt or classifier. It constrains what enters, which capabilities become
eligible, what may leave, which effects commit, and how the enterprise detects and recovers.




                                  Figure 21-1 — Defense-in-depth guardrails architecture

Layer                                   Controls                                         Evidence

                                        Authentication, abuse, injection/jailbreak       Identity, session risk, classifier/version,

                                        poisoning, TTL, correction/deletion

                                        Approved route, instruction integrity, schema,

                                        content safety, evidence, DLP

                                        Least-agentic topology, budgets, deadlines,
Agent/workflow                                                                           Run/task graph and budget ledger
                                        loop/fan-out caps, durable terminals

                                        Subject/workload identity, least privilege,

                                        RBAC/ABAC, purpose/spend/time

                                        Eligible tool, schema/precondition,

                                        proposal/approval, idempotency, reconcile

Trust boundaries and runtime sequence




                                     Figure 21-2 — Agentic security trust boundaries
Trust is not inherited through a chain. User authentication does not authorize the agent; discovery does not authorize a
tool; retrieved text is not instruction; an agent message does not prove peer identity; HTTP success does not prove
business acceptance.




                                                   BOOK v1.0.0   |   PAGE 117
                                                            ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



                              Figure 21-3 — Runtime guardrail and protected-effect sequence
Authenticate subject/workload; classify/minimize input; build authorized labeled context; choose eligible model; validate
structured output; authorize eligible tool and exact arguments; obtain proposal-bound approval when required; execute
through sandbox/gateway with scoped credentials and DLP; reconcile effects and run deterministic acceptance; monitor,
audit, contain, revoke, recover, and learn.

Threat catalog
T-01 — Direct prompt injection
Attack scenario. A user tells the model to ignore policy, reveal instructions, or invoke a forbidden tool.
Enterprise impact. Unauthorized action, disclosure, or policy bypass.
Detection signals. Override phrases, hierarchy conflicts, anomalous tool requests, denial spikes.
Prevention mechanisms. Separate trusted instructions/untrusted input, typed intent, least privilege, adversarial tests.
Runtime controls. Input classifier, eligible-tool policy, schema/output/DLP guards, bounded runtime, approval.
Recovery strategy. Block/degrade, revoke run capability, preserve trace, notify owner, add regression.

T-02 — Indirect prompt injection
Attack scenario. A document, web page, email, or tool result contains instructions aimed at the agent.
Enterprise impact. Compromised decision/effect through authorized retrieval.
Detection signals. Instruction-like retrieved text, risky source, divergence after one source, unusual tool chain.
Prevention mechanisms. Treat retrieval as data, provenance/allowlists, isolation, sanitization, injection tests.
Runtime controls. Context labels, tool eligibility independent of content, evidence checks, least privilege.
Recovery strategy. Quarantine source/snapshot, invalidate context/memory/cache, replay affected runs.

T-03 — Jailbreaks
Attack scenario. Obfuscation, role-play, encoding, multilingual or multi-turn probing evades safeguards.
Enterprise impact. Harmful output or progressive acquisition of restricted information.
Detection signals. Classifier disagreement, encoded payloads, repeated reformulation, near-miss patterns.
Prevention mechanisms. Layered policies/classifiers, rate limits, restricted capabilities, behavior tests.
Runtime controls. Session risk, capability reduction, refusal/redirect, human escalation, output filter.
Recovery strategy. Suspend session/capability, investigate campaign, rotate defenses, add variants.

T-04 — Malicious context
Attack scenario. Authorized-looking context is crafted to mislead or override the task.
Enterprise impact. High-confidence incorrect decisions or manipulated outcomes.
Detection signals. Source disagreement, semantic drift, weak provenance, unsupported claims, metadata outlier.
Prevention mechanisms. ContextManifest, trust tiers, corroboration, signatures, freshness.
Runtime controls. Risk-weighted retrieval, claim-evidence verifier, abstain/escalate on conflict.
Recovery strategy. Quarantine artifact, invalidate snapshot/cache, correct downstream decisions.

T-05 — Retrieval/RAG poisoning
Attack scenario. Poisoned documents/chunks enter or are promoted in the corpus/index.
Enterprise impact. Systematic misinformation, exfiltration, or unsafe actions across runs.
Detection signals. Ingestion anomaly, rank manipulation, source drift, failures tied to new source.
Prevention mechanisms. Signed/approved ingestion, source/index separation, ACL, provenance, poisoning tests.
Runtime controls. Source/snapshot/ACL filter, anomaly ranking, evidence diversity/corroboration.
Recovery strategy. Remove data, rebuild trusted index, invalidate caches, replay affected runs.

                                                  BOOK v1.0.0   |   PAGE 118
                                                             ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE




T-06 — Memory poisoning
Attack scenario. A user, document, tool, or model creates malicious durable memory.
Enterprise impact. Persistent compromise across sessions and future decisions.
Detection signals. Unusual writes, instruction-like memory, corrections, failures tied to one record.
Prevention mechanisms. Candidate-write gate, purpose/scope/TTL, provenance, verification, user control.
Runtime controls. Confidence/freshness checks, no authority from memory, read/write audit.
Recovery strategy. Quarantine/delete record and derivatives, notify, replay, tighten eligibility.

T-07 — Tool misuse
Attack scenario. The model selects the wrong tool or unsafe arguments.
Enterprise impact. Corruption, financial loss, privacy violation, disruption.
Detection signals. Low tool precision, argument/precondition failures, denied calls, effect anomaly.
Prevention mechanisms. Narrow catalog, typed schemas, semantic constraints, risk labels, contract tests.
Runtime controls. Policy/precondition, preview, idempotency, approval, effect ledger, acceptance.
Recovery strategy. Pause tool, revoke credentials, reconcile/compensate, repair contract/tests.

T-08 — Excessive agency
Attack scenario. Agent has excessive tools, time, budget, autonomy, or delegation.
Enterprise impact. Runaway cost, unintended effects, large blast radius.
Detection signals. Long-tail steps/fan-out, budget exhaustion, no-progress loops, broad unused scope.
Prevention mechanisms. Least-agentic design, budgets, tool slicing, depth/fan-out limits, approval.
Runtime controls. Admission/stop conditions, capability leases, circuits, human escalation.
Recovery strategy. Pause/terminate, revoke, reconcile effects, redesign topology and budgets.

T-09 — Privilege escalation
Attack scenario. Agent or child expands resource, action, purpose, spend, or time scope.
Enterprise impact. Cross-tenant or high-impact unauthorized access.
Detection signals. Delegation expansion, audience mismatch, policy denials, unusual role assumption.
Prevention mechanisms. Subject+workload identity, monotonic delegation, RBAC/ABAC, short-lived credentials.
Runtime controls. Reauthorize each hop, intersect scope, just-in-time lease, gateway/tool enforcement.
Recovery strategy. Revoke, isolate, audit/repair resources, fix policy and delegation tests.

T-10 — Data exfiltration
Attack scenario. Sensitive data is sent to unauthorized model, tool, agent, URL, or channel.
Enterprise impact. Regulatory breach, IP loss, customer harm.
Detection signals. Egress anomaly, unknown domain/tool, high-entropy output, cross-region route.
Prevention mechanisms. Classify, minimize/tokenize, egress allowlist, provider terms, private endpoints, DLP.
Runtime controls. Egress proxy, network/DNS policy, destination auth, output DLP, volume limits.
Recovery strategy. Block, revoke keys, contain, preserve evidence, breach workflow.

T-11 — Sensitive-data leakage
Attack scenario. PII/secrets appear in responses, logs, traces, caches, or evaluations.
Enterprise impact. Privacy/security incident and credential compromise.
Detection signals. DLP/canary-secret hit, redaction failure, sensitive cache/log, access anomaly.
Prevention mechanisms. Minimize, structured redaction/tokenization, secret scan, encryption, retention.



                                                  BOOK v1.0.0   |   PAGE 119
                                                           ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



Runtime controls. Input/output/log guard, protected payload references, role-based trace access.
Recovery strategy. Rotate secrets, delete/repair copies, rights/incident process, improve detectors.

T-12 — Insecure agent-to-agent communication
Attack scenario. Agents accept unauthenticated tasks, artifacts, callbacks, or delegation.
Enterprise impact. Spoofing, confused deputy, leakage, cross-tenant effect.
Detection signals. Unknown issuer, signature/audience failure, replay, task/artifact mismatch.
Prevention mechanisms. Mutual workload identity, signed envelope, audience/tenant, nonce/idempotency, schema.
Runtime controls. Agent gateway validates identity, authority, deadline, provenance, cancellation.
Recovery strategy. Reject/quarantine, revoke trust, audit/reconcile, rotate credentials.

T-13 — Malicious MCP servers
Attack scenario. Server advertises deceptive tools, changes behavior, steals data, injects output.
Enterprise impact. Credential theft, tool misuse, persistent injection, supply-chain compromise.
Detection signals. Manifest/schema drift, unsigned version, unexpected egress, anomalous output.
Prevention mechanisms. Approved registry, signing/provenance, sandbox, network policy, version pinning.
Runtime controls. MCP gateway filters discovery, scopes credentials, validates output, records effects.
Recovery strategy. Disable/quarantine version, revoke, inspect traces/effects, restore trusted version.

T-14 — Unsafe code execution
Attack scenario. Generated/retrieved code runs with host, network, secret, or persistence access.
Enterprise impact. RCE, theft, lateral movement, resource abuse.
Detection signals. Unexpected syscall/egress/file, resource spike, prohibited import, escape signal.
Prevention mechanisms. Avoid when possible; ephemeral sandbox, read-only base, no secrets, allowlists, signed
deps.
Runtime controls. Time/resource/syscall/network/file controls, scanning, artifact validation.
Recovery strategy. Kill/isolate, revoke, preserve forensic evidence, patch/rotate base.

T-15 — Identity spoofing
Attack scenario. Request/tool call misrepresents user, workload, tenant, or approver.
Enterprise impact. Unauthorized access and non-repudiation failure.
Detection signals. Issuer/audience/token mismatch, impossible actor chain, unsigned context.
Prevention mechanisms. Strong auth, workload identity, signed context, channel binding, approver auth.
Runtime controls. Revalidate at gateways/tools; never trust model-supplied identity fields.
Recovery strategy. Revoke sessions, invalidate approvals, audit/repair effects, strengthen proofing.

T-16 — Supply-chain attacks
Attack scenario. Compromised SDK, package, model, Skill, container, prompt, evaluator, or pipeline enters release.
Enterprise impact. Widespread execution, hidden behavior, leakage, corrupted assurance.
Detection signals. Hash/signature mismatch, dependency drift, provenance gap, anomalous build/egress.
Prevention mechanisms. Locks, SBOM, signed provenance, trusted builders/signers, scanning, immutable registry.
Runtime controls. Admission verifies digest/provenance; runtime pins artifact and limits privilege/egress.
Recovery strategy. Revoke artifact/key, halt/rollback, scope releases, rotate secrets, disclose.




                                                 BOOK v1.0.0   |   PAGE 120
                                                           ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE




Incident response
Use Detect → Contain → Revoke → Reconcile → Eradicate → Recover → Learn. Containment can pause a tool, MCP
server, model route, Skill, memory namespace, tenant, or agent. Revoke credentials, capabilities, approvals, artifacts,
signing keys, and trust. Reconcile which effects actually occurred; never infer from the model's final message. Restore a
known release and authoritative state, then create privacy-reviewed regression cases.

Transition to Chapter 22
The threat model is established; Chapter 22 applies it to context, retrieval, memory, and state—the data surfaces most
likely to carry hidden instructions.

                                                                        ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE




22 — Context Engineering, RAG, Memory, and
State
  NORTHSTAR AT THIS POINT As runs grow, prompts become a mixture of chat, workflow state, retrieved
  documents, summaries, and learned preferences. Northstar separates these domains and records exactly which
  authorized projection reached each model call.



  DESIGN DECISION Treat prompts as disposable authorized projections; keep canonical state, knowledge, artifacts,
  and memory in distinct stores.




What this chapter delivers
Assemble authorized context from distinct state and knowledge domains and govern reusable memory as a data
product.

Solution blueprint
Figure 14. Context Engineering, Memory, and State.
State owner                 Trust boundary                Scale unit                      Failure containment         Core contracts

Context Service; workflow   authorized sources to model   tenant · source · context                                   ContextManifest ·
                                                                                          one context projection
remains canonical           prompt                        budget                                                      MemoryRecord · EvidenceRef




The context build pipeline
Northstar assembles context in stages: parse the request, calculate source candidates, enforce tenant and resource
authorization, fetch immutable versions, classify and sanitize content, deduplicate, rank, allocate tokens, produce
ordered references, and record a ContextManifest. Only the final authorized boundary materializes bytes into the model
request.
Instructions have a trust hierarchy. System and application instructions are code-like release artifacts. Skill instructions
are pinned capability artifacts. User requests express intent within authority. Retrieved documents and tool outputs are
untrusted evidence. Content cannot promote itself to a higher trust role by saying “ignore previous instructions.”
Memory is not a bag of embeddings. Long-term memory creation is a protected data write with owner, scope,
provenance, confidence, policy basis, TTL, correction, and deletion. Canonical business state always wins over a
memory summary.

Context, workflow state, memory, and enterprise knowledge

                                                                           “Memory” often conflates prompts, conversation history, task state,
Problem                                                                    preferences, learned facts, documents, and recovery data—causing
                                                                           leakage, stale decisions, and unreproducible runs.

                                                                           Each type has different scope, consistency, privacy, retention,
Why it is difficult                                                        freshness, and retrieval semantics; models can misclassify or invent
                                                                           memories.

                                                                           Full transcript replay; summarization; graph checkpoints; key/value
Design alternatives                                                        memory; vector memory; knowledge graph; source-grounded
                                                                           retrieval; event-sourced state.

Context assembly pipeline
214.     Start with agent instructions and policy-owned constraints from pinned versions.
215.     Load canonical workflow state and unresolved obligations—not the raw event history.
216.     Select the smallest recent conversation window needed for continuity.
217.     Retrieve enterprise knowledge only after identity/tenant/ACL filtering; attach source, timestamp, classification,
    and confidence metadata.
218.     Retrieve long-term memory through a separate policy gate; prefer verified facts and user-approved preferences.
219.     Remove duplicates, stale items, secrets, and content outside the task purpose; summarize only with links to
    originals.
220.     Label each segment as instruction, user\_input, enterprise\_data, tool\_result, or memory; the model must not
    promote data into instructions.
221.     Record a context-manifest hash on the model span so the call can be reconstructed without logging sensitive
    plaintext by default.

                                                        ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE




Detailed design — APIs, data model, and scalability
Component boundaries
The Conversation Service owns turns; the Workflow Projection Service owns canonical run state; the Context Assembler
creates an ephemeral model-input projection; the Retrieval Broker queries governed knowledge; the Memory Service
owns approved cross-run memory; the Provenance Service tracks lineage; and the Compaction Service produces
source-linked summaries. No component can silently promote a conversation statement into workflow truth or long-term
memory.

High-level APIs
API                                                         Purpose and key semantics

                                                            Build a token-bounded context from pinned instructions, run state,
POST /v1/context:assemble                                   conversation, knowledge, and memory under a policy and data
                                                            budget

                                                            Return the canonical typed state projection and state\_version;
GET /v1/runs/{run\_id}/state
                                                            separate from transcript/history

                                                            Append an immutable user/agent/tool turn with provenance,
POST /v1/conversations/{id}/turns
                                                            classification, and client idempotency key

                                                            Hybrid search with subject+actor context, source allowlist, freshness,
POST /v1/knowledge:search
                                                            filters, top-k, and required citations

                                                            Propose/write a governed memory with scope, provenance,
POST /v1/memories                                           confidence, consent, TTL, and deduplication key; policy may require
                                                            review

                                                            Search only memories visible to the subject/purpose/run; returns
GET /v1/memories:search
                                                            confidence and source lineage

PATCH /v1/memories/{memory\_id}                             Correct via optimistic version; preserves previous version and reason

                                                            Create deletion tombstone and enqueue removal from indexes,
DELETE /v1/memories/{memory\_id}
                                                            caches, summaries, and derived artifacts

                                                            Produce a source-linked summary/checkpoint under a target token

                                                            budget; never overwrites source turns

Retrieval and memory consistency
      The source system revision is authoritative. Index entries are disposable projections and must carry source revision,
       ACL pointer, ingestion time, and deletion state.
      Search performs tenant/source pruning, exact ACL filtering, hybrid lexical/vector scoring, reranking, freshness
       filtering, and diversity/deduplication before context selection.
      A memory write is a governed business operation: validate type, scope, consent, provenance, contradiction,
       retention, and sensitivity. Derived guesses default to run-scoped working memory, not long-term memory.
      Correcting or deleting a source creates lineage events that invalidate affected memories, summaries, embeddings,
       model caches, and evidence packages according to retention/legal-hold policy.

Scalability and token economics
      Partition conversations and memories by tenant + subject/team; partition knowledge by tenant + source + region.
       Use routing metadata so a query touches only eligible partitions.
      Store content in object storage and metadata in relational/search stores. Context APIs exchange references; only
       the assembler materializes bytes for the authorized model call.
      Run ingestion as an asynchronous CDC/event pipeline with separate extract, classify, ACL, chunk, embed, and
       index stages. Track independent cursors so failed embedding does not block ACL deletion.
      Cache retrieval candidates by tenant, source revision, normalized query, policy scope, and embedding/reranker
       version; re-run exact authorization on cache hit.
      Allocate a fixed context budget before retrieval. Use maximum marginal relevance/source diversity, per-source caps,
       and evidence-quality thresholds rather than filling the entire window.
      Compact long conversations asynchronously and incrementally. Keep recent turns plus source-linked summaries;
       rebuild summaries when covered inputs change.
      Enforce deletion SLOs across primary metadata, vector/search indexes, caches, and derived summaries.
       Tombstones must outlive the longest replica/cache lag to prevent resurrection.

Grounding, corrections, and evidence boundary
Claim or correction                        Grounded statement                             Source IDs

                                           Context and memory need purpose limitation,
                                           consent or other legal basis where
                                           applicable, data-subject request handling,
Privacy lifecycle                          deletion propagation, legal-hold precedence,   NIST-GAI; GDPR
                                           residency, provider retention controls, and
                                           proof that derived summaries/indexes were
                                           handled too.


Evidence anchors: NIST-GAI; GDPR. Full URLs, authority, version, and scope appear in Appendix M.

Pattern reference — Memory
    PROBLEM The system should reuse useful information across turns or runs without confusing stale summaries with
    canonical truth or retaining data indefinitely.



Architecture and flow
Working memory is run-scoped; long-term memory is a governed write with subject, scope, provenance, confidence,
policy basis, TTL, correction, deletion, and retrieval authorization.
222.         Identify a candidate reusable fact.
223.         Validate evidence, subject, purpose, consent or policy basis, and sensitivity.
224.         Write a versioned memory record with TTL and provenance.
225.         Retrieve only after authorization and trust labeling.
226.         Correct, supersede, expire, or delete through propagation workflows.
Use when                                                              Do not use when

                                                                      Do not persist transient chain-of-thought, secrets, unverified
Use for stable preferences, repeated domain facts, or learned
                                                                      hypotheses, protected data without basis, or facts already
operational hints with clear value and governance.
                                                                      authoritative elsewhere.



Trade-offs
Choice                                                                Consequence

Continuity                                                            Creates privacy, staleness, correction, and deletion obligations.

Reduced context cost                                                  May lose nuance or introduce summary drift.



Failures and controls
Failure                                                               Control

Stale memory overrides state                                          Canonical state precedence and freshness checks.

Cross-tenant leakage                                                  Tenant-partitioned keys and authorization before retrieval.

Deletion gaps                                                         Lineage graph, tombstones, cache invalidation, and rebuild.



Minimum observability
      memory ID and version
      source provenance
      scope and tenant
      policy basis
      confidence and TTL
      retrieval and deletion events

Cross-boundary artifact adoption policy
PYTHON · implementation excerpt
PYTHON

1        from __future__ import annotations
2
3        from datetime import datetime
4        from pydantic import BaseModel, Field
5
6
7        class ArtifactDescriptor(BaseModel):
8            artifact_id: str
9            tenant_id: str
10           data_classes: frozenset[str]
11           region: str
12           allowed_purposes: frozenset[str]
13           provenance_digest: str
14           expires_at: datetime
15           legal_hold: bool = False
16
17
18       class AdoptionPolicy(BaseModel):
19           tenant_id: str
20           allowed_data_classes: frozenset[str]
21           allowed_regions: frozenset[str]
22           purpose: str
23           trusted_provenance_digests: frozenset[str]
24
25
26       def authorize_artifact_adoption(
27           artifact: ArtifactDescriptor, policy: AdoptionPolicy, *, now: datetime
28       ) -> None:
29           if artifact.tenant_id != policy.tenant_id:
30               raise PermissionError("cross-tenant artifact adoption denied")
31           if not artifact.data_classes <= policy.allowed_data_classes:
32               raise PermissionError("artifact classification exceeds policy")
33           if artifact.region not in policy.allowed_regions:
34               raise PermissionError("artifact region is not allowed")
35           if policy.purpose not in artifact.allowed_purposes:
36               raise PermissionError("artifact purpose is not allowed")
37           if artifact.provenance_digest not in policy.trusted_provenance_digests:
38               raise PermissionError("artifact provenance is not trusted")
39           if now > artifact.expires_at and not artifact.legal_hold:
40               raise PermissionError("artifact expired")




Debugging playbook
227.     Reconstruct one ContextManifest without relying on logged plaintext.
228.     Compare canonical state with summaries and working memory.


                                                 BOOK v1.0.0   |   PAGE 127
                                                                ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



229.       Trace a corrected or deleted fact through indexes, cache, memory, and artifacts.

Production checklist
✓ Conversation, workflow state, working memory, long-term memory, knowledge, artifacts, telemetry, and audit are
separate domains.
✓ Authorization occurs before content materialization.
✓ Context records ordered references, hashes, trust labels, decisions, versions, redactions, and token allocation.
✓ Memory creation is a governed write with scope, provenance, confidence, lawful basis, TTL, correction, and deletion.
✓ Final implementation and deletion propagate through indexes, caches, summaries, memories, contexts, and
artifacts.

230.       Design a token budget across instructions, state, evidence, memory, and reserve.
231.       Define the lifecycle of a reusable supplier preference memory.

23 — Identity, RBAC/ABAC, and Delegated
Authorization
     Evidence note. The delegated-authority examples use OAuth token exchange, resource indicators, and protected-
     resource metadata as protocol building blocks; deployment still requires workload-specific issuer, audience, proof,
     revocation, and policy controls. [R08][R09][R10]

  NORTHSTAR AT THIS POINT A procurement user starts the run, a platform agent reasons, a connector calls an
  ERP, and a specialist agent receives a child task. Northstar must preserve both initiating subject and acting workload
  without turning a broad user token into ambient authority.



  DESIGN DECISION Carry subject plus actor identity; exchange for short-lived last-hop credentials only after exact
  resource authorization.




What this chapter delivers
Carry subject and actor identity end to end and prove that every delegated grant is narrower.

Solution blueprint
Figure 15. Identity, RBAC/ABAC, and Delegated Authorization.
State owner                   Trust boundary               Scale unit                       Failure containment          Core contracts

                                                                                                                         CapabilityEnvelope ·
Policy Engine and security-   subject/actor to protected   policy shard · tenant ·
                                                                                            one decision and lease       PolicyDecision ·
token service                 resource                     resource
                                                                                                                         CredentialLease




Subject, actor, and purpose
Every protected request carries the initiating subject, acting workload, purpose, tenant, capability envelope, and
trace/run identifiers. RBAC supplies coarse role membership; ABAC evaluates resource, purpose, data class, risk,
region, time, and current state. Neither the model nor a remote agent invents these attributes.
A child grant is derived by intersection, not by copying the parent token. It shortens lifetime and delegation depth and
narrows actions, resources, data classes, regions, risk, and budget. Immediately before a tool call, the gateway
canonicalizes the exact resource and reauthorizes it. Only then does a security-token service mint a short-lived
audience-bound credential lease for the connector.
Authorization caches must fail safely. Keys include tenant, subject, actor, resource version or ACL epoch, purpose,
policy version, action, and relevant context. Revocation and policy updates invalidate or bypass stale positive decisions.
After a long wait, authorization is recomputed.

Authorization decision
Every retrieval, delegation, tool call, memory write, and publication evaluates a tuple:
allow(subject, actor, action, resource, tenant, purpose, data\_class, environment, risk, run\_id, time, device/session
assurance, requested\_scope)
The effective permission is the intersection—not the union—of all applicable grants. Explicit deny wins. Cache only low-
risk decisions for short periods and invalidate on identity or policy change.

Identity propagation sequence
Data-plane controls
    Resolve tenant before any search; enforce row, document, and field-level authorization before retrieval or
     embedding results reach the model.
    Separate indexes, encryption keys, namespaces, queues, caches, and rate limits by tenant or by risk tier; use
     database row-level security as defense in depth.
    Bind credentials to audience and preferably sender; rotate frequently; store only handles in workflow state; refresh
     after long waits and re-check revocation.
    Use workload identity (for example SPIFFE/SVID or cloud workload federation) so workers do not carry static
     secrets.
    Run the policy decision point independently from the LLM. OPA/Rego, Cedar/Verified Permissions, or a comparable
     engine can express RBAC/ABAC and emit replayable decision logs.
    Treat document-level ACL changes as index invalidation events. Never assume a vector store filter alone is
     sufficient if the source of truth denies access.

Consistency and revocation policy
      Writes, R3/R4 actions, secret access, external publication, and post-wait resumes require a current online decision.
       Low-risk reads may use a short, policy-bounded cache if the source and tenant permit it.
      Cache keys include tenant, subject, actor, action, canonical resource, purpose, policy digest, entitlement version,
       and assurance—not merely user and role.
      Group/ACL/policy changes emit invalidation events. The authorization service maintains a high-water mark per
       source; if freshness exceeds the action’s tolerance, deny or query the source of truth.
      Connector calls preserve both subject and actor in downstream claims or audit metadata. When a source cannot
       express delegated identity, the gateway records the mapping and compensating control; it does not pretend service-
       account access is user delegation.

Scalability and isolation
      Keep PDP instances stateless and horizontally scalable; distribute signed policy bundles and keep tenant/resource
       relationship data in a partitioned authorization store.
      Batch authorization for search results and field projections to avoid one network call per chunk. Apply coarse
       candidate pruning first, then exact resource checks before returning content.
      Partition entitlement and resource data by tenant + source, with regional replicas inside residency boundaries. Large
       enterprise group expansion should use indexed relationships rather than embedding group lists in tokens.
      Isolate connectors by source, tenant tier, and risk. Apply per-connector queues, circuit breakers, concurrency caps,
       and rate-limit feedback to planners.
      Use independent encryption keys and audit streams for high-risk tenants. A shared physical service is acceptable
       only when tenant binding is enforced at every API, data partition, cache key, trace, and credential exchange.
      Model a regional IdP/STS outage explicitly: existing low-risk read grants may follow a documented grace policy,
       while new or mutating grants fail closed and runs checkpoint for later resume.

Grounding, corrections, and evidence boundary
Claim or correction                          Grounded statement                                  Source IDs

                                             Workload identity proves the actor;
                                             delegation proves whose authority and
Non-human identity                           purpose it uses. Rotate and revoke both             RFC8693; MCP-AUTH
                                             credentials and capability grants. A valid
                                             service identity is not sufficient authorization.


Evidence anchors: RFC8693; RFC8707. Full URLs, authority, version, and scope appear in Appendix M.

Implementation reference
Capability-envelope subset proof
PYTHON · implementation excerpt
    PYTHON

1            from __future__ import annotations
2
3            from dataclasses import dataclass
4            from datetime import datetime, timezone
5
6
7            def _resource_within(child: str, parent: str) -> bool:
8                if parent.endswith("://"):
9                    return child.startswith(parent)
10               normalized = parent.rstrip("/")



                                                       BOOK v1.0.0     |   PAGE 132
                                                           ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



11          return child == normalized or child.startswith(normalized + "/")
12
13
14      @dataclass(frozen=True)
15      class CapabilityEnvelope:
16          grant_id: str
17          subject_id: str
18          actor_id: str
19          actions: frozenset[str]
20          resource_prefixes: frozenset[str]
21          purposes: frozenset[str]
22          data_classes: frozenset[str]
23          regions: frozenset[str]
24          maximum_risk: int
25          maximum_cost_usd: float
26          delegation_depth: int
27          expires_at: datetime
28
29          def assert_current(self, now: datetime | None = None) -> None:
30              current = now or datetime.now(timezone.utc)
31              if self.expires_at <= current:
32                  raise PermissionError("capability envelope expired")
33
34
35      def derive_child(
36          parent: CapabilityEnvelope,
37          child: CapabilityEnvelope,
38          *,
39          now: datetime | None = None,
40      ) -> CapabilityEnvelope:
41          """Prove monotonic narrowing; actor identity may change, subject may not."""
42
43          parent.assert_current(now)
44          if child.subject_id != parent.subject_id:
45              raise PermissionError("subject cannot change")
46          if not child.actions <= parent.actions:
47              raise PermissionError("actions broadened")
48          if not child.purposes <= parent.purposes:
49              raise PermissionError("purposes broadened")
50          if not child.data_classes <= parent.data_classes:
51              raise PermissionError("data classes broadened")
52          if not child.regions <= parent.regions:
53              raise PermissionError("regions broadened")
54          if child.maximum_risk > parent.maximum_risk:
55              raise PermissionError("risk ceiling raised")
56          if child.maximum_cost_usd > parent.maximum_cost_usd:
57              raise PermissionError("cost ceiling raised")
58          if child.delegation_depth >= parent.delegation_depth:
59              raise PermissionError("delegation depth was not reduced")
60          if child.expires_at > parent.expires_at:
61              raise PermissionError("lifetime extended")
62          for resource in child.resource_prefixes:
63              if not any(_resource_within(resource, allowed) for allowed in parent.resource_prefixes):
64                  raise PermissionError("resource scope broadened")
65          return child




Debugging playbook
232.    Trace subject, actor, purpose, grant, policy version, and credential lease end to end.
233.    Canonicalize the exact resource and recompute child subset checks.
234.    Simulate role revocation during a wait and verify post-wait denial.

Production checklist
✓ Both initiating subject and acting workload cross every protected boundary.
✓ Delegated authority is a bounded capability envelope, never a reusable bearer credential.
✓ Child grants narrow actions, resources, purpose, data class, region, risk, time, budget, and delegation.
✓ Exact action and resource are reauthorized at the gateway before a last-hop credential is minted.

                                                 BOOK v1.0.0   |   PAGE 133
                                                                  ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



✓ Revocation, freshness, cache invalidation, post-wait revalidation, and tenant isolation are first-class.

Exercises
235.       Create a capability envelope for a read-only specialist task.
236.       Write tests that attempt purpose, resource, region, risk, and time escalation.

24 — Tool Security, Effects, Idempotency, and
Approval
  NORTHSTAR AT THIS POINT The ERP times out after Northstar requests supplier creation. Retrying might
  duplicate the supplier; treating the call as failed might cause conflicting remediation. The platform records an
  unknown effect and reconciles before continuing.



  DESIGN DECISION Use one logical effect key, intent hash, expected version, downstream request ID, receipt, and
  reconciliation state per mutation.




What this chapter delivers
Make external writes replay-safe through manifests, exact authorization, logical effect keys, preconditions, receipts, and
reconciliation.

Solution blueprint
Figure 16. Tool Security, Effects, Idempotency, and Approval.
State owner                    Trust boundary             Scale unit                       Failure containment       Core contracts

Tool Gateway, Effect Ledger,   agent intent to external                                                              EffectRecord · EffectReceipt ·
                                                          logical effect key · connector   one business effect
domain writer                  mutation                                                                              ReconciliationTask




The effect protocol
A protected mutation moves through Reserved → Executing → Committed, with branches for Declined, Unknown,
Partial, Compensating, Compensated, and InterventionRequired. The logical effect key represents business intent; the
intent hash prevents the key from being reused for a different action. An expected version prevents valid intent from
overwriting newer state.
The gateway persists Executing before the downstream call and includes a stable idempotency key when supported.
Success is not inferred from HTTP 200 alone; a receipt includes downstream resource ID, resulting version, response
hash, timestamp, and verification evidence. After timeout, the state becomes Unknown, dependent actions stop, and a
reconciler queries the downstream system using request ID, idempotency key, or business predicates.
Approval and effect control are complementary. Approval proves a person authorized the proposal; it does not prove the
write happened once. Idempotency prevents duplicate intent; it does not prevent stale overwrite. A robust protected write
often needs all three: proposal-bound approval, idempotency, and optimistic concurrency.

                                                                           discovery can expose dangerous capabilities; schemas and

Invocation behavior:
     Return 200 only for short, completed pure/read operations with no unresolved effect.
     Return 202 plus an operation URL for long-running, queued, browser/code, or mutating work.
     Return a typed policy denial, validation error, capacity response, dependency error, or effect\_unknown; do not
      collapse every failure into free-form text.
