A unique constraint on (tenant\_id, tool\_id, idempotency\_key) protects logical effects. The arguments hash must match
on reuse; the same key with different intent is a conflict, not a retry.

Invocation sequence
Scalability and operations
    Keep registry/discovery/gateway front ends stateless. Cache immutable manifests by digest and distribute them
     regionally; always perform a current authorization check at invocation.
    Partition invocation and effect records by tenant + tool, but route all uses of one logical idempotency key to the same
     consistency domain.
    Use dedicated queues/pools for read connectors, write connectors, browser/code sandboxes, high-risk operations,
     and very slow providers. Enforce fairness and reservations independently.
    Exchange large inputs/outputs through authorized object references. Queue messages carry IDs, hashes,
     classification, and deadlines—not document bodies.
    Apply concurrency and rate limits at organization, tenant, run, tool, connector account, target resource, and provider
     levels. Feed retry-after and breaker state back to the scheduler.
    Prewarm only common sandbox images; use immutable snapshot pools and capped warm capacity. Scrub or
     destroy every lease, and never reuse tenant-writable layers across trust boundaries.
    Publish health changes as events so planners avoid unavailable tools before dispatch. Do not allow a model to
     bypass a breaker by selecting a semantically identical unapproved endpoint.
    Separate gateway availability from connector availability: if a connector is degraded, queue within bounded age or
     return capacity status so the workflow can wait, degrade, or escalate.

Reliability and failure handling
Required analysis                                               Design conclusion

                                                                Preserve correct outcomes despite nondeterministic models, partial
Problem                                                         infrastructure failures, duplicates, rate limits, and uncertain external
                                                                effects.

                                                                A timeout does not reveal whether an external mutation committed;
Why it is difficult                                             retries can amplify harm; model failures may be semantic rather than
                                                                exceptions; cross-system transactions rarely exist.

                                                                Best-effort retries; queue redelivery; distributed transactions;
Design alternatives                                             idempotent activities; effect ledger; saga compensation;
                                                                reconciliation; human exception queue.

                                                                Classify every operation by effect semantics. Use bounded
                                                                exponential backoff with jitter for transient failures; stable
Recommended architecture                                        idempotency keys; outbox/inbox; precondition versions; effect
                                                                receipts; sagas and reconciliation for multi-system changes; circuit
                                                                breakers and provider fallbacks tested in advance.

                                                                  real systems.

                                                                  Duplicate refund; two runs edit the same case; fallback model

                                                                  cycles forever; global retry storm.

                                                                  An invoice agent creates a payment proposal with an idempotency

Enterprise example

Reliability mechanics
    Timeout hierarchy: model/tool request \< activity \< child task \< stage \< overall run. A parent timeout must not
     silently abandon a still-running child.
    Retry ownership: exactly one layer owns retries for a given failure; otherwise SDK, gateway, queue, and workflow
     retries multiply.
    Idempotency: derive keys from tenant + run + logical effect, persist before invocation, and return an effect receipt
     containing downstream request and resource IDs.
    Concurrency: use optimistic versions for records. Use short distributed leases only to coordinate scarce work;
     never hold a lock across model calls or human waits.
    Sagas: define forward and compensating activities. Compensation is a business action, not history deletion; keep
     both in the audit trail.
    Circuit breaking: break by provider/tool/tenant/risk class; expose health to the planner so it selects only evaluated
     alternatives.
    Graceful degradation: degrade from write → draft, deep analysis → partial evidence, premium model → approved
     fallback, live data → clearly labeled cached data. Never degrade authorization, tenant isolation, or audit.




                                                   BOOK v1.0.0     |   PAGE 139
                                                               ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE




Detailed design — APIs, data model, and scalability
Component boundaries
The workflow engine owns retry timing and saga order; the Effect Service owns idempotency and external outcome
state; Outbox/Inbox adapters own atomic publication and consumer deduplication; the Reconciliation Service resolves
unknown outcomes; the Compensation Executor performs domain reversals; the Dependency Health Service owns
breakers; and the Exception Service owns manual intervention cases.

High-level APIs

Effect state machine
Transaction boundaries
     In a local database transaction, update the aggregate/effect state and insert its outbox event together. A publisher
      retries until the broker acknowledges; consumers deduplicate in an inbox before applying state.



                                                     BOOK v1.0.0   |   PAGE 140
                                                                  ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



      An external API call cannot share that transaction. Use preconditions and idempotency, then store/reconcile the
       external receipt. “Exactly once” is a business protocol, not a queue setting.
      A workflow transition that depends on an effect reads the Effect Service’s verified state; a model/tool return string is
       not sufficient proof.
      Compensation is separately authorized against current state and may itself be idempotent, fail, or require human
       approval.

Scalability and resilience
      Partition effect/outbox/inbox data by tenant + logical target/tool. Maintain the idempotency unique index in the same
       shard that accepts begin.
      Stream outbox records by monotonically increasing shard cursor; avoid a single global publisher. Track oldest
       unpublished age and backlog bytes as primary SLOs.
      Run reconcilers as queue workers grouped by downstream provider and strategy. Apply strict per-provider
       concurrency and spread checks with jitter to avoid recovery storms.
      Scope circuit breakers correctly: global provider, region, tenant account, endpoint, or operation. Over-broad breakers
       cause unnecessary outage; over-narrow breakers fail to protect the dependency.
      Bound every retry by attempt, elapsed time, spend, and deadline. A shared retry budget across workflow, SDK,
       gateway, and connector prevents multiplicative retries.
      Separate quarantine storage from live queues. Redrive in controlled batches with canaries; compare
       schema/policy/tool versions and regenerate credentials.
      Replicate effect metadata for DR but preserve one writer for a logical key. After regional failover, reconcile all
       Executing/Unknown effects before releasing dependent mutations.
      Use append-only receipts and state-transition records for audit, plus compact current projections for fast lookup.
       Archive old evidence by retention class without deleting required idempotency tombstones.

Grounding, corrections, and evidence boundary
Claim or correction                          Grounded statement                             Source IDs

                                             Idempotency is scoped to one stable logical
                                             intent. Reusing a key with different
Effect correctness                           arguments is a conflict; retrying an unknown   OUTBOX; CLOUDEVENTS
                                             write is prohibited until reconciliation
                                             establishes committed or absent.


Evidence anchors: OUTBOX; CLOUDEVENTS. Full URLs, authority, version, and scope appear in Appendix M.

Implementation reference
Effect-state ledger
PYTHON · implementation excerpt
    PYTHON

1            from __future__ import annotations
2
3            import hashlib
4            import json
5            from dataclasses import dataclass, replace
6            from typing import Literal
7
8            EffectState = Literal["reserved", "executing", "committed", "declined", "unknown",
9                                  "compensating", "compensated", "intervention_required"]
10
11           class EffectConflict(RuntimeError): pass
12           class EffectOutcomeUnknown(RuntimeError): pass
13
14           @dataclass(frozen=True)
15           class EffectRecord:
16               logical_key: str
17               intent_hash: str
18               state: EffectState
19               receipt: dict[str, object] | None = None
20               downstream_request_id: str | None = None



                                                      BOOK v1.0.0    |   PAGE 141
                                                                ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



21            reconciliation_evidence: dict[str, object] | None = None
22
23       class InMemoryEffectLedger:
24           """Reference implementation; production uses a transactional durable store."""
25           def __init__(self) -> None: self._records: dict[str, EffectRecord] = {}
26           @staticmethod
27           def hash_intent(intent: dict[str, object]) -> str:
28                payload=json.dumps(intent,sort_keys=True,separators=(",",":"),default=str).encode()
29                return "sha256:"+hashlib.sha256(payload).hexdigest()
30           def begin(self, logical_key: str, intent: dict[str, object]) -> EffectRecord:
31                digest=self.hash_intent(intent); existing=self._records.get(logical_key)
32                if existing:
33                    if existing.intent_hash != digest: raise EffectConflict("logical key reused with a different
         intent")
34                    return existing
35                record=EffectRecord(logical_key,digest,"reserved"); self._records[logical_key]=record; return record
36           def _transition(self,key:str,allowed:set[str],state:EffectState,**changes) -> EffectRecord:
37                current=self._records[key]
38                if current.state not in allowed: raise EffectConflict(f"cannot move {current.state} to {state}")
39                updated=replace(current,state=state,**changes); self._records[key]=updated; return updated
40           def mark_executing(self,key:str,request_id:str)->EffectRecord:
41                return self._transition(key,{"reserved"},"executing",downstream_request_id=request_id)
42           def mark_committed(self,key:str,receipt:dict[str,object])->EffectRecord:
43                return self._transition(key,{"executing","unknown"},"committed",receipt=dict(receipt))
44           def mark_unknown(self,key:str)->EffectRecord:
45                return self._transition(key,{"executing"},"unknown")
46           def mark_declined(self,key:str,receipt:dict[str,object]|None=None)->EffectRecord:
47                return self._transition(key,{"reserved"},"declined",receipt=dict(receipt or {}))
48           def
         reconcile(self,key:str,*,outcome:Literal["committed","absent","unprovable"],evidence:dict[str,object])-
         >EffectRecord:
49                if outcome=="committed": return self._transition(key,
         {"unknown"},"committed",receipt=dict(evidence),reconciliation_evidence=dict(evidence))
50                if outcome=="absent": return self._transition(key,
         {"unknown"},"declined",reconciliation_evidence=dict(evidence))
51                return self._transition(key,
         {"unknown"},"intervention_required",reconciliation_evidence=dict(evidence))
52           def get(self,key:str)->EffectRecord:return self._records[key]




Debugging playbook
237.       Locate the logical effect key, intent hash, expected version, and downstream request ID.
238.       Classify the current state as reserved, executing, committed, absent, partial, or unknown.
239.       Block dependent actions until reconciliation produces evidence.

Production checklist
✓ Commands and facts are distinct and events use versioned envelopes.
✓ Transactional outbox and inbox close state and message gaps.
✓ Each retry class has one bounded owner.
✓ Every capability has an immutable manifest covering interface, security, risk, effects, runtime, ownership, and lifecycle.
✓ Discovery is minimal and exact resource authorization happens at invocation.

Exercises
240.       Extend the effect ledger with partial and compensated states.
241.       Design a reconciliation algorithm for an API without native idempotency keys.

25 — Sandboxing, Egress, Data Security, and
Governance
     Evidence note. SBOM inventory and SLSA build provenance answer different supply-chain questions. Privacy
     obligations depend on jurisdiction; the GDPR source is cited only for EU legal context, not as universal legal
     advice. [R17][R18][R39]

  NORTHSTAR AT THIS POINT A supplier document contains a hidden instruction to upload contracts to an external
  site. Northstar assumes the model may obey it and relies on sandbox, egress, capability, credential, DLP, and
  approval controls to prevent harm.



  DESIGN DECISION Design so prompt injection may manipulate reasoning but cannot reach unauthorized data,
  credentials, network destinations, or effects.




What this chapter delivers
Contain a manipulated model with capability, credential, execution, network, supply-chain, and governance controls.

Solution blueprint
Figure 17. Sandboxing, Egress, Data Security, and Governance.
State owner                  Trust boundary              Scale unit                      Failure containment     Core contracts

Sandbox service, egress      untrusted content/code to   sandbox pool · destination ·                            SandboxProfile · EgressGrant
                                                                                         one sandbox and grant
proxy, governance registry   network and data            tenant                                                  · UseCaseRecord




Security assumption: the model may be manipulated
Northstar’s security case remains valid even if a supplier document successfully changes the model’s behavior. The
compromised model can only choose among the minimal tools and data already eligible for the current node. It cannot
read credentials, create arbitrary clients, reach an unapproved host, weaken policy, bypass approval, or mark an effect
committed.

Defense layers
242.    Capability: narrow tools, resources, risk, time, spend, and delegation.
243.    Data: authorize before retrieval, label provenance and trust, minimize fields, and enforce tenant partitions.
244.    Credential: short-lived, audience-bound, action- and resource-scoped leases at the last hop.
245.    Execution: immutable sandbox profiles, non-root isolation, quotas, ephemeral storage, and no ambient secrets.
246.    Network: default-deny egress, canonical destinations, DNS and redirect controls, byte/time limits, and DLP.
247.    Effects: policy, approval, idempotency, preconditions, receipts, and reconciliation.
248.    Supply chain: signed agents, Skills, prompts, tools, images, dependencies, policy, schemas, and attestations.
249.    Governance: use-case inventory, accountable owner, risk tier, exception workflow, incident process, review
    cadence, and evidence.
A governance record should name business purpose, autonomy, data, tools, external effects, human controls, model and
vendor dependencies, region, risk classification, evaluations, release, owner, approvers, exceptions, incidents, review
date, and retirement plan. Governance is an executable lifecycle, not a slide deck.

Security must be tested against the current OWASP agentic threat taxonomy and organization-specific abuse cases, but
compliance with a list is not proof of safety. Prompt-injection defenses remain probabilistic; architecture must limit what a
successfully manipulated model can see and do.

Detailed design — APIs, data model, and scalability
Component boundaries
The Content Security Gateway scans/classifies inbound and retrieved content; policy enforcement points mediate each
trust boundary; the Secret/Credential Broker prevents secret exposure; the Sandbox Control Plane attests and leases
isolated execution; the Egress Gateway resolves and filters outbound destinations; DLP inspects structured and
unstructured payloads; the Peer/Tool Trust Registry validates supply-chain identity; and the Kill-Switch Service revokes
capabilities independently of agent code.

High-level APIs

                                                                Scan content reference for malware, active content, secrets, PII,

Defense-in-depth request path
Sandboxing and egress rules
    Default to ephemeral non-root workloads, read-only base images, isolated writable overlays, no host
     mounts/devices, syscall filtering, resource quotas, no cloud metadata, and default-deny network.
    Canonicalize and resolve destinations at the egress proxy to defend against redirect, DNS-rebinding, IPv6/private-
     range, URL-parser, and alternate-protocol bypasses.
    Bind egress grants to workload identity, method, canonical destination, purpose, payload classification/size/hash
     constraints, expiry, and maximum uses.
    Inject secrets at the last possible hop through memory/file descriptor/sidecar mechanisms and redact
     process/environment dumps. The model and generated code receive capability handles, not secret values.
    Scan outputs and derived artifacts before memory writes, model reuse, cross-agent transfer, or publication;
     untrusted tool/peer responses remain tainted.

Scalability and availability
    Place lightweight PEPs, kill-switch caches, and signed policy bundles close to gateways/workers. Keep central
     policy/control writes low volume and strongly audited; distribute invalidations through a high-priority channel.
    Stateless content/DLP workers scale by bytes and document complexity, with separate pools for archive expansion,
     OCR, code, images, and low-latency request checks. Set decompression and recursion limits.
    Scale sandboxes by profile-specific pools. Admission considers warm capacity, startup latency, risk isolation,
     regional capacity, and egress-proxy limits—not only worker CPU.
    Shard egress gateways by region/risk/tenant tier and maintain shared deny intelligence without sharing payloads. A
     gateway outage fails closed for new external writes and checkpoints affected workflows.
    Stream security telemetry asynchronously where possible, but block a protected action until required
     inspection/authorization completes. Audit delivery failure cannot silently permit an R3/R4 effect.
    Use bounded caches only for immutable attestations and low-risk allow decisions. Revocation, high-risk actions,
     secrets, and external publication require current checks.
    Quarantine suspect peers/tools/artifacts by digest so healthy versions continue operating. Kill switches target the
     narrowest safe scope and are tested as part of disaster exercises.

Grounding, corrections, and evidence boundary
Claim or correction                      Grounded statement                               Source IDs

                                         NIST AI RMF is voluntary and under revision
                                         as of 2026; ISO/IEC 42001:2023 is a
                                         management-system standard; OWASP is
                                                                                          NIST-RMF; ISO42001; OWASP-AGENT; EU-
Authority of governance sources          community security guidance; the EU AI Act
                                                                                          AI-ACT
                                         is law with phased applicability. None
                                         substitutes for jurisdiction-specific legal
                                         analysis.

                                         Pre-wire kill switches by tenant, agent
                                         release, tool, connector, model route, and
Incident control                         data source. A kill switch must stop new risky   OWASP-AGENT
                                         work while preserving evidence and effect
                                         reconciliation.

Implementation reference
Default-deny URL authorization
PYTHON · implementation excerpt
PYTHON

1        from __future__ import annotations
2
3        import ipaddress
4        import socket
5        from urllib.parse import urlsplit, urlunsplit
6
7
8        def authorize_url(raw_url: str, allowed_hosts: frozenset[str]) -> tuple[str, tuple[str, ...]]:
9            parsed = urlsplit(raw_url)
10           if parsed.scheme.lower() != "https" or parsed.username or parsed.password:
11               raise PermissionError("invalid egress URL")
12           if not parsed.hostname:
13               raise ValueError("host required")
14           host = parsed.hostname.encode("idna").decode("ascii").rstrip(".").lower()
15           port = parsed.port or 443
16           if port != 443 or host not in allowed_hosts:
17               raise PermissionError("destination outside egress grant")
18           addresses: set[str] = set()
19           for item in socket.getaddrinfo(host, port, type=socket.SOCK_STREAM):
20               address = ipaddress.ip_address(item[4][0])
21               if not address.is_global:
22                   raise PermissionError(f"non-public address blocked: {address}")
23               addresses.add(address.compressed)
24           canonical = urlunsplit(("https", f"{host}:443", parsed.path or "/", parsed.query, ""))
25           return canonical, tuple(sorted(addresses))



Scoped operational kill switch
PYTHON · implementation excerpt
PYTHON

1        from __future__ import annotations
2
3        from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, model_validator
4
5
6        class KillSwitchRule(BaseModel):
7            tenant_id: str | None = None
8            agent_version: str | None = None
9            tool_version: str | None = None
10           model_route: str | None = None
11           reason: str = Field(min_length=1)
12
13           @model_validator(mode="after")
14           def at_least_one_scope(self) -> "KillSwitchRule":
15               if not any((self.tenant_id, self.agent_version, self.tool_version, self.model_route)):
16                   raise ValueError("kill switch requires a scope")
17               return self
18
19           def blocks(self, *, tenant_id: str, agent_version: str, tool_version: str | None,
20                      model_route: str) -> bool:
21               checks = (
22                   self.tenant_id in (None, tenant_id),
23                   self.agent_version in (None, agent_version),
24                   self.tool_version in (None, tool_version),
25                   self.model_route in (None, model_route),
26               )
27               return all(checks)
28
29
30       class RecoveryObjectives(BaseModel):
31           tier: str
32           rto_seconds: int = Field(gt=0)

                                                                   ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



33            rpo_seconds: int = Field(ge=0)
34            maximum_degraded_minutes: int = Field(ge=0)
35            minimum_safe_capabilities: frozenset[str]




Debugging playbook
250.       Follow the tainted content to every capability and network boundary.
251.       Verify no credentials entered prompt, generated code, logs, or sandbox files.
252.       Review DLP, redirect, DNS, egress, artifact, and kill-switch evidence.

Production checklist
✓ The design remains safe even if the model follows a malicious instruction.
✓ Credentials, arbitrary network access, production clients, and authorization authority stay outside model reach.
✓ Untrusted content is labeled, minimized, and constrained through capability, sandbox, egress, DLP, and effect
controls.
✓ Prompts, models, tools, peers, packages, images, schemas, and attestations are pinned and verified.
✓ Targeted emergency denies, revocation, isolation, quarantine, and incident evidence are available.

Exercises
253.       Threat-model an injected supplier PDF using an attack tree.
254.       Write a default-deny egress policy for the code-execution sandbox.

Technical review questions
Question                                                              Strong answer

                                                                      Prompt injection is semantic and probabilistic; a model may follow
Why can prompt engineering not be the primary security boundary?      malicious content, so deterministic identity, capability, data, sandbox,
                                                                      and network controls must contain it.

                                                                      Treat the model as potentially compromised while still preventing
What is the correct threat-model assumption for the agent?            unauthorized data access, credential retrieval, privileged effects, and
                                                                      exfiltration.

                                                                      Immutable sandbox profile, non-root isolation, resource limits,
How would you secure generated code or browser automation?            ephemeral storage, no ambient secrets, default-deny egress,
                                                                      approved artifacts, monitoring, and destruction.

                                                                      Validate subject and actor policy, exchange for a short-lived

                                                                      final hop, and never expose the secret to prompts or generated code.

                                                                      Narrow scopes, emergency-deny precedence, independent high-
What makes a useful kill-switch design?                               priority distribution, measurable propagation, governed issuer and

26 — Observability and Tracing with MLflow
     Evidence note. OpenTelemetry GenAI semantic conventions remain in Development and have moved to a
     dedicated repository; isolate them behind an internal mapping layer. Phoenix is one implementation option for
     OpenInference/OTel-based tracing and evaluation. [R19][R23]

  NORTHSTAR AT THIS POINT A run completes slowly and expensively. Operators can see a final answer but
  cannot tell whether the delay came from queues, retrieval, policy, model turns, retries, human dwell, or a slow
  connector. Northstar links the entire trajectory.



  DESIGN DECISION Adopt OpenTelemetry-compatible causal traces and MLflow 3.15.x for diagnostics; retain
  complete protected audit evidence separately.




What this chapter delivers
Connect workflow, agent, model, retrieval, policy, tool, approval, effect, and human activity into one causal trajectory.

Solution blueprint
Figure 18. Observability and Tracing with MLflow.
State owner                      Trust boundary         Scale unit                      Failure containment          Core contracts

Trace pipeline; separate audit   sensitive runtime to   trace shard · regional                                       TraceContext · SpanLink ·
                                                                                        one span/export batch
ledger                           operations access      collector                                                    AuditEvent




One causal trajectory
A production trace links admission, workflow tasks, AgentRuntime activities, model calls, retrieval, policy decisions, tool
invocations, approvals, effects, waits, remote tasks, operator commands, and terminal acceptance. Long-running runs
require stable domain IDs and span links because queues, retries, resumptions, and fan-out do not form one in-process
call stack.
Northstar uses OpenTelemetry-compatible context propagation and MLflow 3.15.x tracing. mlflow.openai.autolog() can
capture OpenAI calls, while explicit spans cover platform-owned boundaries. Required attributes include tenant-safe run
and task IDs, release versions, workflow build, model route, tool and policy versions, risk class, token and cost usage,
effect state, retry owner, artifact references, and stop reason.

Privacy and reliability
Raw prompts, retrieved documents, credentials, and protected tool payloads do not automatically belong in traces.
Record governed artifact references, hashes, classifications, and redaction metadata. Regional collectors buffer, redact,
batch, and export. Diagnostic telemetry may be sampled; protected audit events are written separately with
completeness and integrity controls plus monitoring.

Debugging method
Start with the violated outcome or SLO, identify the critical path, then find the first divergent state transition or decision.
Distinguish queue time, execution time, human dwell, retry delay, and reconciliation time. Compare the run manifest and
context/evidence versions before comparing final prose.

Observability, debugging, testing, and evaluation
Required analysis                                                        Design conclusion

                                                                         Explain and improve a distributed probabilistic trajectory, not merely
Problem
                                                                         log the final text.

Trace model
Adopt OpenTelemetry-compatible context and GenAI semantic conventions, with organization-specific attributes where
standards are still evolving. A run is the root span; workflow nodes, model calls, retrievals, policy checks, tool/A2A calls,
approvals, checkpoints, and compensation are child spans or linked asynchronous traces.

Do not treat hidden chain-of-thought as required observability. Record concise decision summaries, selected
alternatives, evidence references, structured plans, policy outputs, and tool trajectories. This is more stable, safer, and
more operationally useful.

Trace and replay semantics
      Propagate W3C traceparent/tracestate across HTTP, queues, MCP, and A2A. Use span links when work is triggered
       asynchronously or crosses an independently rooted remote trace.
      Record model route/version, prompt/context hashes, tool/agent versions, policy decision IDs, artifact/effect
       references, retry/cancel/replay flags, cost, and timing. Do not require hidden chain-of-thought.
      Operational replay reuses recorded activity outputs and cannot create live effects. A fork with changed
       model/prompt/tool is a new run and explicitly mocks or reauthorizes external access.
      Deterministic correctness checks outrank model graders. Store grader prompt/model/version, raw score evidence,
       and inter-rater calibration so a later drift analysis is possible.

Scalability, retention, and cost
      Decouple application latency from backend analytics with regional collectors, bounded queues, batching,
       compression, retry, and encrypted spill. Protected effect/audit events follow a loss-intolerant path distinct from
       sampled diagnostics.
      Partition trace storage by tenant + time; index only operationally useful low-cardinality fields. Put high-cardinality
       attributes and content in columnar/object storage with controlled query paths.
      Use tiered sampling: 100% metadata for runs/effects/policy/approvals; 100% detailed traces for failures, security
       findings, canaries, R3/R4, and operator-selected runs; adaptive/statistical detail for routine success.
      Move older traces from hot index to compressed object storage, preserving searchable summaries and evidence
       pointers. Retention differs for telemetry, evaluation fixtures, and legal/audit records.
      Run evaluations on dedicated queues with tenant/candidate budgets, reproducible seeds, provider-rate awareness,
       and early stop when statistical or hard safety failure criteria are met.
      Compute metrics from events/spans through streaming aggregation rather than querying raw traces for dashboards.
       Control label cardinality; never use user IDs, prompts, or URLs as metric labels.
      Preserve sampling weights and selection reasons for online estimates. Compare by task/risk/data/source/model
       slices so improvements in average score cannot hide a critical regression.
      Monitor the observability system itself: collector drop rate, redaction failure, trace completeness, broken links, ingest
       lag, hot-store cost, evaluator disagreement, and dataset leakage.

Grounding, corrections, and evidence boundary
Claim or correction                         Grounded statement                             Source IDs

                                            mature, but the dedicated OpenTelemetry
Telemetry maturity                          GenAI semantic conventions are still           OTEL-GENAI
                                            Development. Version the mapping and
                                            retain a stable internal event schema.

10        def configure_observability() -> None:
11            mlflow.set_tracking_uri(os.environ.get("MLFLOW_TRACKING_URI", "http://localhost:5000"))
12            mlflow.set_experiment("northstar-supplier-onboarding")
13            # Captures OpenAI calls; add explicit spans around platform-owned workflow,
14            # policy, approval, and effect boundaries.
15            mlflow.openai.autolog()
16
17        def traced_step(run_id: str, supplier_id: str) -> dict[str, str]:
18            mlflow.update_current_trace(tags={"run_id": run_id, "risk_class": "R2"})
19            with mlflow.start_span(name="load_authorized_context", span_type=SpanType.RETRIEVER) as span:
20                span.set_inputs({"supplier_id": supplier_id})
21                context_manifest_id = f"ctx:{run_id}:1"
22                span.set_outputs({"context_manifest_id": context_manifest_id})
23            return {"status": "ready_for_approval", "context_manifest_id": context_manifest_id}




Debugging playbook
255.       Start at the SLO violation and walk the critical path span by span.
256.       Correlate queue, workflow, model, retrieval, tool, approval, and effect timing.
257.       Compare diagnostic trace completeness with required audit evidence.

Production checklist
✓ Run, workflow, agent, model, retrieval, policy, tool, approval, effect, and human spans share stable causal identifiers.
✓ Asynchronous relationships use span links and domain IDs.
✓ Sensitive content is excluded, tokenized, or access-controlled.
✓ Collector outage does not stop protected workflow state or required audit evidence.
✓ SLOs expose critical-path latency, cost, errors, retries, human dwell, and unknown effects.

27 — Evaluation, Testing, and Debugging with
MLflow
     Evidence note. The evaluation architecture maps to current capabilities documented by MLflow, DeepEval,
     Ragas, LangSmith, and OpenAI. Tool names and APIs are examples; the durable contract is dataset + release
     graph + scorer versions + trace/effect evidence. [R20][R21][R22][R24][R25][R26]

    NORTHSTAR AT THIS POINT A prompt change improves average answer quality but introduces one unauthorized
    tool trajectory in a low-volume region. Aggregate accuracy hides the regression; slice-aware trajectory evaluation
    blocks the release.



    DESIGN DECISION Gate releases on immutable datasets, executable trajectory checks, calibrated judges, slices,
    failures, and baseline comparisons.




What this chapter delivers
Evaluate outcomes and trajectories using immutable datasets, executable scorers, calibrated judges, failure simulation,
and release gates.

Solution blueprint
Figure 19. Evaluation, Testing, and Debugging with MLflow.
State owner               Trust boundary           Scale unit                        Failure containment      Core contracts

Evaluation Service and    production evidence to   dataset version · scorer pool ·   one case and candidate   EvaluationCase · Feedback ·
release gate              development corpus       slice                             release                  ReleaseDecision




Evaluation-driven development
An agent evaluation case includes input, initial state, user simulation or messages, expected outcome properties,
allowed and forbidden tools, required evidence, maximum budgets, failure injections, and slice tags. The result includes
final output, complete trajectory, artifacts, policy decisions, effects, human events, latency, cost, and terminal state.
MLflow 3.15.x supports GenAI evaluation, trace-based scorers, code-based scorers, LLM judges, human feedback, and
OpenAI Agent tracing integration. Version 3.15.2 adds immutable evaluation-dataset versions and scorer ensembles
within the requested 3.15 line. This book pins 3.15.2; newer overall MLflow releases may exist and require separate
compatibility validation.

Evaluation pyramid
     Contract and unit: schemas, reducers, compilers, capability subsets, idempotency, deterministic acceptance.
     Simulation: multi-turn users, tool fixtures, clock, failures, approvals, and remote peers.
     Trajectory: correct tools, order, evidence, policy, budgets, handoffs, waits, and effects.
     Threat: prompt injection, exfiltration, confused deputy, cross-tenant, SSRF, malicious artifact, approval tampering.
     Reliability: retries, timeouts, duplicates, out-of-order events, unknown effects, cancellation, replay, regional
      recovery.
     Performance: queue age, tokens, tool calls, latency, cost per success, concurrency, fairness.
     Canary and production: bounded exposure, online monitors, human feedback, failure mining, rollback.
Use executable scorers wherever the expected property is computable. Calibrate model judges against human labels,
measure variance and bias, version their complete configuration, and never make them the sole authority for security or
transaction correctness.

Use deterministic validators as the gold standard where possible. LLM-as-judge is useful for nuanced rubrics but must be
calibrated against humans, blinded to candidate identity, tested for variance/bias, and never be the sole gate for safety-
critical correctness. Measure repeated-run reliability (pass^k or equivalent), not just best-of-one success.

Implementation reference
MLflow trajectory evaluation
PYTHON · implementation excerpt
 PYTHON

1         from __future__ import annotations
2         import os
3         from typing import Literal
4         import mlflow
5         from mlflow.entities import Feedback, SpanType, Trace
6         from mlflow.genai import scorer
7         from mlflow.genai.judges import make_judge
8         from mlflow.genai.scorers import Correctness, Guidelines, ToolCallCorrectness, ToolCallEfficiency
9
10        def no_unknown_effects(trace: Trace) -> Feedback:
11            unknown = [
12                span for span in trace.search_spans()
13                if span.attributes.get("effect.state") == "unknown"
14            ]
15            return Feedback(
16                name="no_unknown_effects",
17                value=not unknown,
18                rationale="No unresolved effects" if not unknown else f"{len(unknown)} unknown effects remain",
19            )
20
21        def evaluate_agent(predict_fn, dataset: list[dict[str, object]]):
22            return mlflow.genai.evaluate(
23                data=dataset,
24                predict_fn=predict_fn,
25                scorers=[
26                    Correctness(),
27                    Guidelines(name="grounded", guidelines="Every material claim cites authorized evidence."),
28                    ToolCallCorrectness(),
29                    ToolCallEfficiency(),
30                    no_unknown_effects,
31                    tool_trajectory_judge,
32                ],
33            )




Debugging playbook
260.      Reproduce the failing trace with exact dataset, release, model route, tool, and policy versions.
261.      Find the first divergent decision rather than only comparing final text.
262.      Slice scores by risk, region, tool, failure mode, and tenant class.

Production checklist
✓ All run components link into one causal trajectory.
✓ Operational telemetry, restricted content, and complete immutable audit evidence are separated.
✓ Evaluation covers outcome, trajectory, reliability, security, latency, cost, human load, and operations by slice.
✓ Behaviorally relevant artifacts are pinned in a release bundle and promoted through executable gates.
✓ Use-case inventory names owners, authority, data, vendors, controls, exceptions, incidents, review dates, and
evidence.

Exercises
263.      Create a five-case immutable evaluation dataset with trajectory expectations.
264.      Implement a deterministic scorer that rejects unknown effects and missing approvals.

Technical review questions
Question                                                                Strong answer

                                                                        A plausible answer can hide unauthorized retrieval, wrong tools,
Why is final-answer evaluation insufficient for agents?                 excessive cost, retry waste, unsafe handoffs, duplicate effects, or
                                                                        missing approval; evaluate the complete trajectory.

                                                                        Telemetry diagnoses and may be sampled; audit proves required
How do telemetry and audit differ?                                      protected decisions and effects with stronger completeness, integrity,
                                                                        access separation, and retention.

                                                                        For nuanced quality with versioned rubrics, human calibration, bias
When are model judges appropriate?                                      tests, repeated runs, and blinding—not as sole authority for security,
                                                                        transactions, or computable correctness.

                                                                        Agent and prompt, runtime adapter, workflow build, model route and
What belongs in a release bundle?                                       fallbacks, tool and policy versions, schemas, images, knowledge
                                                                        strategy, evaluations, rollout, and rollback policy.

                                                                        Stop new assignment, restore the prior environment binding, keep
How do you roll back without breaking active runs?                      build-compatible workers for pinned histories, reconcile side effects,
                                                                        and migrate only through an explicit tested plan.

lifecycle.

28 — Continuous Learning and Improvement
Architecture
Closed-loop capability
Continuous improvement joins production evidence to versioned change and release. It does not let an agent rewrite its
prompt, memory policy, permissions, evaluator, or code and then certify itself.

Runtime adaptation versus controlled offline improvement




                          Figure 28-2 — Runtime adaptation and offline improvement boundary
Runtime adaptation selects only pre-approved routes/fallbacks, reduces optional context, sheds load, stops a loop, asks
for input, escalates, or rolls back inside a fixed envelope. It cannot expand permissions, purpose, data access, spend,
tools, or model allowlists. Offline improvement creates a new immutable release and may change prompts, context/RAG,
memory, tools, skills, model selection, routing, deterministic code, agent topology, workflows, and system architecture
after evaluation and approval.

Evaluation ecosystem and metrics
OpenTelemetry/OpenInference traces provide portable telemetry. DeepEval and Ragas cover trajectory, component,
tool, RAG, faithfulness, and goal metrics. MLflow supports datasets, evaluators, human/programmatic feedback,
experiments, and monitoring. Phoenix supports traces, annotations, datasets, prompt experiments, and replay.
LangSmith supports offline/online evaluation and production-trace-to-dataset workflows. Normalize all into platform-
owned EvaluationResult; no framework score is release authority.
Measure task success and deterministic acceptance, correctness, faithfulness, hallucination/unsupported-claim rate,
tool-selection precision/recall/F1, argument correctness, retrieval relevance/precision/recall/freshness/evidence
coverage, plan quality/adherence, step efficiency, p50/p95/p99 latency by component, tokens, cost per request/accepted
outcome, retry amplification, failure/unknown effect, human correction/override/escalation/fatigue, user satisfaction, and
business outcome.

Regression and safe automation
Every material failure/correction becomes a candidate case after privacy/security/purpose review. Reduce to smallest
reproducible cause, define expected outcome/invariants/trajectory/cost/latency, version dataset/evaluator, and run in CI,
staging, shadow, and canary. Automate capture, approved redaction/scoring, anomaly detection, clustering, draft
recommendations, tests, sandbox/canary, halt, and rollback within envelope. Require human governance for
permission/policy/data/memory purpose/protected effects/model allowlist/topology/architecture/evaluator truth/training
data/exceptions/wider promotion. Prohibit expanding authority, disabling controls, editing audit evidence, changing
approval requirements, or promoting self-authored behavior.

Transition to Chapter 29
The improvement loop proposes and validates change; Chapter 29 assigns decision rights, risk tiers, approvals,
exceptions, and accountability.

                                                 ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE

                            GOVERNANCE

    lifecycle oversight. It connects technical release controls to an operating model rather than
                            treating governance as a review gate at the end.

29 — Governance, Risk, and Operating Model
    Evidence note. NIST AI RMF and its Generative AI Profile are voluntary risk guidance; ISO/IEC 42001 specifies
    an AI management system; the EU AI Act source provides official implementation context. Applicability and legal
    obligations require qualified jurisdiction-specific review. [R12][R13][R14][R40]

Federated operating model
Governance makes decisions explicit, proportional, evidence-based, and reversible. It accelerates safe delivery with
reusable risk tiers, policy, review paths, and evidence rather than sending every prompt edit to one committee.




                              Figure 29-1 — Federated governance with centralized control services

Assess incorrect-outcome impact, sensitive data, protected effects, legal relevance, autonomy, exposure, scale,
reversibility, multi-agent/tool complexity, and human detectability. Risk is not the model name.

Policy, review, and cadence
Principles define non-negotiable outcomes; standards define reusable contracts; policy-as-code decides eligible models,
data, tools, actions, budgets, and approvals; application configuration selects within the envelope; exceptions are
scoped, monitored, owned, and expiring. Review outcome/acceptance, deterministic boundary, state/effect ownership,
identity/delegation, context/memory, tool/MCP/A2A trust, threats, evaluation truth, observability/privacy, recovery,
release, capacity/cost, human oversight, and portability.
Operate per run (admission/policy/effects/acceptance), daily/weekly (SLO, quality, safety, cost, incidents, evaluator
health), per release (classification, regression, security/privacy, compatibility, canary/rollback), quarterly (portfolio
value/risk and platform roadmap), and annually/material change (policy, law, threat, recovery, independent assurance).

Transition to Chapter 30
Governance defines who may release change; Chapter 30 makes releases immutable, promotable, observable,
reversible, and recoverable.

                         verified—not merely generated—outcomes.

30 — Deployment, Versioning, CI/CD, and
Recovery
     Evidence note. The incident lifecycle aligns with NIST SP 800-61 Rev. 3’s integration of incident response into
     cybersecurity risk management; agent-specific containment and recovery remain workload-defined. [R42]

    NORTHSTAR AT THIS POINT A new release changes a tool schema and workflow decision. Active runs still
    contain the old event history. Northstar deploys compatible workers, pins release manifests, canaries new starts, and
    rolls back assignment without rewriting history.



    DESIGN DECISION Release immutable bundles with compatibility evidence; roll back new assignment while active
    runs remain pinned to compatible code.




What this chapter delivers
Ship immutable release bundles through compatibility checks, canaries, workflow-aware rollback, and regional recovery.

Solution blueprint
Figure 21. Deployment, Versioning, CI/CD, and Recovery.
State owner               Trust boundary          Scale unit                     Failure containment   Core contracts

                                                                                                       ReleaseManifest ·
Release Registry and      candidate artifact to   release cohort · workflow
                                                                                 one release cohort    CompatibilityMatrix ·
compatible worker pools   production assignment   build · cell
                                                                                                       RolloutPlan




The release is the behavior
A production release includes more than application code: agent and Skill versions, prompts and instructions, SDK
adapter, workflow build, model route and fallbacks, tool and policy versions, schemas, sandbox image, knowledge
strategy, evaluation suite, dashboards, runbooks, rollout, and rollback. Northstar records these in an immutable
ReleaseManifest and pins the manifest to each run.
CI verifies types and contracts, deterministic replay, policy and grant subsets, tool and effect semantics, prompt and
trajectory regressions, threat cases, performance, container signatures, provenance, and migration compatibility. CD
promotes the exact same signed artifacts through shadow, canary, tenant cohort, and regional stages.

Rollback and active runs
Rollback changes the release assigned to new work. Active workflows continue on compatible workers or use an explicit
tested migration. Rewriting history or silently changing a tool schema underneath an active run is not rollback. High-risk
changes may pause affected runs until a compatible worker or reviewed migration is available.
Regional recovery fences the old writer, restores durable state with a compatible build, reconciles executing and
unknown effects, verifies signals and task ownership, advances placement atomically, and resumes by risk tier.
Recovery is rehearsed with evidence, not inferred from replica health.

Deployment architecture
Recommended production topology
Deployment guidance
     Control plane: globally available registry, release, evaluation, and governance services publish signed immutable
      bundles. Production workers pull approved versions; they do not edit control-plane definitions.


                                                  BOOK v1.0.0     |   PAGE 170
                                                              ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



    Regional execution planes: keep prompts, state, artifacts, retrieval indexes, queues, traces, and model endpoints
     in the required residency boundary. Route each run to a home region and avoid transparent cross-region migration
     of in-flight state.
    High availability: run gateways, policy decision points, workflow front ends, and queue workers across zones. The
     workflow history and relational stores need tested backup/restore and defined regional failover; “multi-region” without
     replay tests is not a resilience claim.
    Isolation: namespaces are not the only boundary. Combine tenant-aware authorization, row-level security, separate
     encryption keys, network policies, workload identity, queue quotas, and—where risk requires—dedicated
     accounts/clusters.
    Worker classes: separate lightweight model/tool orchestration from browser/code execution, GPU inference, data
     processing, and high-risk connectors. Each class has an immutable image, resource limits, egress policy, and
     workload identity.
    Secrets: fetch just in time from a broker or exchange identity for a short-lived credential. Never bake secrets into
     images, prompts, tool manifests, checkpoints, or environment dumps.
    Release: deploy agent and workflow bundles using GitOps-style promotion, canaries, feature flags, and automated
     rollback. Keep old workflow code available until all compatible in-flight runs complete or migrate.
    Disaster recovery: define RPO/RTO separately for run history, business effects, artifacts, indexes, telemetry, and
     audit. Reconcile effects against source systems after recovery before resuming mutations.

Promotion and continuous-improvement loop
Governance controls
    Inventory every deployed agent, use case, owner, business purpose, autonomy level, tools, data classes, users,
     vendors, regions, SLOs, and risk tier.
    Map controls to NIST AI RMF Govern/Map/Measure/Manage, ISO/IEC 42001 management-system processes,
     security/privacy standards, and applicable sectoral rules. As of August 2026, most EU AI Act rules and enforcement
     apply; legal classification must be use-case specific.
    Require documented intended use, prohibited use, foreseeable misuse, human-oversight design, evaluation
     evidence, residual risk acceptance, and rollback owner before release.
    Produce an immutable evidence package per material run: request, identities, purpose, versions, policy decisions,
     source provenance, plan revisions, tool effects, approvals, output, metrics, incidents, and retention class.


                                                 BOOK v1.0.0    |   PAGE 172
                                                                    ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



     Separate telemetry access from business-data access; redact by default and support legal hold, deletion, residency,
      and customer-managed keys.
     Document explanations at the right level: evidence and rules for business decisions; trajectory summaries for
      operations; model internals are neither necessary nor sufficient.
     Never auto-train or rewrite production policies/prompts directly from live conversations. Curate, de-identify, review,
      evaluate, and promote changes through the same lifecycle.

Compatibility and long-running execution
    Every run records a resolved bundle. A deployment change affects new runs only unless an explicit, tested migration
     updates an active run.
    Workflow compatibility includes replay behavior, activity names/schemas, state migrations, signals, and effect
     semantics—not just API request shape.
    Tool/model fallback is itself a versioned route with eval evidence. “Same family” or “newer model” is not proof of
     compatibility.
    Policies can impose emergency deny outside the pinned bundle; the run records both the original policy and the
     current higher-precedence denial.
    Knowledge snapshots may be “as-of,” “current under freshness policy,” or a recorded set of source revisions. The
     manifest states which reproducibility level applies.

Scalability and control-plane resilience
    Registry/release/governance writes are low volume and strongly consistent. Reads are high volume: distribute
     signed bundles/manifests through regional caches/object storage keyed by digest.
    Workers verify bundle signatures/digests locally and keep a last-known-good cache. New high-risk runs fail closed if
     current policy/kill-switch state cannot be verified.
    Avoid a synchronous global registry call on every activity. Resolve once at admission, pass content addresses, and
     refresh only explicitly dynamic health/authorization data.
    Index dependency edges to answer “what uses this version?” before deprecation, incident containment, or
     vulnerability response. Run impact analysis asynchronously but from a consistent snapshot.
    Roll out by tenant/use case/risk/region cohorts with bounded canary volume. Automated rollback consumes
     streaming SLO/eval signals but never rewrites historical manifests.
    Keep release metadata, signatures, policies, and governance evidence replicated with tested backup/restore.
     Separate normal promotion permissions from emergency deny/rollback authority.
    Archive old artifacts only after all active-run, replay, audit, legal-hold, and rollback dependencies expire. Retain
     digest tombstones permanently enough to prevent ambiguous reuse.

Implementation reference
Release compatibility check
PYTHON · implementation excerpt
 PYTHON

1         from __future__ import annotations
2
3         from pydantic import BaseModel
4
5
6         class ReleaseManifest(BaseModel):
7             release_id: str
8             agent_versions: tuple[str, ...]
9             skill_pack_versions: tuple[str, ...]
10            workflow_build: str
11            sdk_version: str
12            model_route_version: str
13            tool_versions: tuple[str, ...]
14            policy_version: str
15            schema_versions: tuple[str, ...]
16            evaluation_report_id: str
17
18
19        def compatible(run: ReleaseManifest, worker: ReleaseManifest) -> bool:
20            return (
21                run.workflow_build == worker.workflow_build
22                and run.sdk_version == worker.sdk_version
23                and set(run.tool_versions) <= set(worker.tool_versions)
24                and set(run.schema_versions) <= set(worker.schema_versions)
25            )



Release attestation verification
PYTHON · implementation excerpt
 PYTHON

1         from __future__ import annotations
2
3         import hashlib
4         from pydantic import BaseModel, Field
5
6
7         class ReleaseAttestation(BaseModel):
8             release_id: str
9             artifact_sha256: str
10            signer_identity: str
11            source_revision: str
12            builder_identity: str
13            sbom_ref: str
14            provenance_ref: str
15            dependency_lock_digest: str
16            evaluation_evidence_refs: tuple[str, ...] = Field(min_length=1)
17



                                                     BOOK v1.0.0    |   PAGE 175
                                                                       ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



18
19       def verify_release_artifact(
20           content: bytes,
21           attestation: ReleaseAttestation,
22           *,
23           trusted_signers: frozenset[str],
24           trusted_builders: frozenset[str],
25       ) -> None:
26           digest = "sha256:" + hashlib.sha256(content).hexdigest()
27           if digest != attestation.artifact_sha256:
28               raise ValueError("artifact digest mismatch")
29           if attestation.signer_identity not in trusted_signers:
30               raise PermissionError("untrusted release signer")
31           if attestation.builder_identity not in trusted_builders:
32               raise PermissionError("untrusted build platform")
33           if not attestation.sbom_ref or not attestation.provenance_ref:
34               raise ValueError("SBOM and provenance are required")




Debugging playbook
265.       Resolve the exact release manifest and worker compatibility for the run.
266.       Replay history before migrating workflow code.
267.       Verify rollback stopped new assignment without changing active history.

Production checklist
✓ Client and workflow contracts remain stable across SDKs, providers, engines, and connectors.
✓ Idempotency, concurrency, asynchronous operations, deadlines, pagination, events, errors, and artifacts are
consistent.
✓ Each authoritative record and consistency boundary has one owner and store.
✓ Partitions, queues, quotas, fairness, backpressure, and cells isolate workloads.
✓ Compatibility, schema migration, fencing, regional recovery, and effect reconciliation are designed before deployment.

31 — Cost, Latency, Capacity, and Reliability
Optimization
    Evidence note. Little’s Law relates average items in a stable system to arrival rate and average time in system.
    The capacity equations in this chapter are planning approximations and must be calibrated by load tests. [R43]

  NORTHSTAR AT THIS POINT Demand triples at quarter end. Northstar must protect deadlines and tenant fairness
  while controlling tokens, tool calls, queue depth, and human load. Optimization is judged by verified business
  outcomes, not by token price alone.



  DESIGN DECISION Optimize cost per verified success through shorter trajectories, selective context, deliberate
  routing, bounded concurrency, and safe degradation.




What this chapter delivers
Optimize verified outcome quality, latency, cost, and capacity without weakening safety or evidence.

Solution blueprint
Figure 22. Cost, Latency, Capacity, and Reliability Optimization.
State owner                Trust boundary            Scale unit                      Failure containment   Core contracts

                                                                                                           BudgetLease ·
Admission controller and   tenant demand to scarce   workload class · tenant ·
                                                                                     one admitted run      CapacityPermit ·
budget service             shared capacity           queue
                                                                                                           DegradationDecision




Optimize the trajectory, not the token
The useful objective is cost and latency per verified successful outcome, constrained by quality, safety, reliability,
and compliance. A cheaper model can be more expensive if it causes more turns, retrievals, retries, escalations, or
human review. First remove unnecessary work: prefer deterministic paths, smaller eligible tool sets, early acceptance,
selective retrieval, artifact reuse, and bounded parallelism.

Latency and cost budget
Allocate an end-to-end deadline across admission, queue, context, model, tools, human dwell, and reserve. Each child
task inherits a narrower budget. Track expected and worst-case tokens, tool calls, external charges, sandbox minutes,
and human minutes. The budget service issues permits; workers cannot exceed the lease by continuing to reason.

Capacity and fairness
Separate queues by workload and risk class. Weighted fair scheduling prevents a large tenant from starving others.
Concurrency permits reflect the actual scarce resource: model route, vendor rate limit, browser pool, code sandbox,
database writer, or approver queue. Backpressure starts at admission rather than after every subsystem is overloaded.

Safe degradation
Under pressure, Northstar can defer nonurgent work, produce a draft rather than commit, reduce optional context, use
an evaluated lower-cost route, serialize optional fan-out, or request human scheduling. It never disables authorization,
tenant isolation, approval, effect verification, or required audit. Every optimization is tested by slice, canaried, reversible,
and observable.




                                                     BOOK v1.0.0      |   PAGE 178
                                                            ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE




Scalability, performance, and cost
Required analysis                                               Design conclusion

                                                                Scale bursty, heterogeneous, quota-bound work while controlling
Problem
                                                                latency, spend, fairness, noisy neighbors, and long-lived idle runs.

                                                                Model and tool limits differ; agent fan-out is dynamic; a slow branch
Why it is difficult                                             holds joins; retries amplify load; GPU and sandbox workloads have
                                                                very different resource profiles.

                                                                Synchronous monolith; generic job queue; serverless functions;
Design alternatives                                             Kubernetes worker pools; actor systems; workflow-task queues;
                                                                provider-managed runtime.

                                                                Separate the stateless control plane from queue-driven execution
                                                                pools. Partition queues by workload/risk/tenant/region; use fair
Recommended architecture                                        scheduling and admission budgets; autoscale on backlog age and
                                                                service time; apply hierarchical quotas and backpressure before
                                                                model providers. Suspend idle workflows without workers.

                                                                More pools improve isolation and tuning but increase operations.
                                                                Managed runtimes reduce undifferentiated work while increasing
Trade-offs
                                                                vendor dependency and potentially weakening cross-vendor trace
                                                                and state portability.

                                                                One tenant consumes all quota; fan-out explosion; retry storm; head-
Failure scenarios                                               of-line blocking; model quota is exhausted while workers scale
                                                                uselessly; sandbox image cold starts dominate latency.

                                                                A month-end finance workload uses priority queues and reserved
                                                                model quota for close-critical tasks, separate pools for OCR/code
Enterprise example

                                                                than failing all runs.



Scaling topology
Capacity and concurrency controls
    Hierarchical token buckets: organization → environment → tenant → agent → user → run → provider/tool.
    Queue partitions and worker pools by latency class, risk, region/residency, sandbox profile, model family, and data
     classification.
    Concurrency keys for scarce resources or records; use optimistic concurrency for data and semaphores for quotas.
    Backpressure on predicted service time, queue age, provider quota, and spend—not CPU alone. KEDA-style event-
     driven autoscaling is appropriate for queue workers.
    Bounded fan-out and speculative execution. Cancel losing branches once a validated result wins; account for
     cancellation lag.
    Fair scheduling with reserved capacity for critical workflows and protection against a single tenant or recursive agent
     tree.
    Prewarmed sandboxes only for latency-sensitive workloads; immutable images, snapshot pools, and aggressive idle
     reclamation for the rest.

Cost/performance optimization order
270.     Remove unnecessary agency: replace stable decisions with code, rules, search, or classifiers.
271.     Reduce work: incremental recomputation, event correlation, cached deterministic results, delta retrieval, and
    early exit on acceptance criteria.
272.     Reduce context: structured state projections, source-linked summaries, retrieval top-k tuning, deduplication,
    and tool-result compression.
273.     Route models: choose the cheapest evaluated model for the node; escalate by difficulty, uncertainty, or failed
    validation—not by habit.
274.     Parallelize selectively: only independent, sufficiently valuable branches; cap fan-out and straggler time.
275.     Cache safely: key by model/version, prompt hash, normalized input, tenant, authorization scope, policy, and
    knowledge snapshot. Do not share sensitive cache entries across principals.
276.     Batch: embeddings, classification, and offline evals where latency permits.
277.     Optimize the ACI: precise tool schemas and smaller tool sets reduce retries and wrong calls more reliably than
    ever-longer prompts.


                                                  BOOK v1.0.0   |   PAGE 179
                                                           ENTERPRISE AGENTIC AI ARCHITECTURE & DEVELOPER PLATFORM GUIDE



278.     Use spend-aware planning: each plan estimates cost and reserves budget; report marginal value of optional
    branches.
279.     Measure cost per accepted outcome: token price alone ignores retries, human review, failed trajectories, and
    tool charges.

Detailed design — APIs, data model, and scalability
Component boundaries
The Admission Controller predicts resource/risk and accepts, defers, or rejects work; the Hierarchical Quota Service
owns organization-to-run limits; the Queue/Scheduler owns priority and fairness; the Worker Lease Service owns
assignment/heartbeat; Provider Limiters translate external quotas into dispatch permits; the Capacity Controller scales
pools; and the Usage/Cost Ledger attributes reserved and actual resources.

Scheduling algorithm
280.     Admission estimates critical-path latency, maximum concurrency, worst/expected cost, required capabilities, and
    regional constraints.
281.     Reserve hard budget and scarce capacity only for the near execution horizon; use forecasts for later nodes.
282.     Place work into the narrowest compatible queue. Weighted fair queuing selects tenant/class, then earliest
    deadline/priority within it.
283.     Acquire provider/tool/sandbox permits before leasing work; if unavailable, keep the item queued rather than
    occupying a worker.
284.     Lease with bounded heartbeat. Expiry makes work eligible for redelivery, while activity/effect idempotency
    protects correctness.
285.     Emit usage immediately, reconcile provider invoices asynchronously, and release unused reservations when
    nodes terminate.
286.     Backpressure admission when oldest age, predicted deadline miss, approval backlog, provider quota, or budget
    risk exceeds policy.

Scalability mechanics
   Scale each workload pool independently. A practical concurrency estimate is arrival\_rate × mean\_service\_time /
    target\_utilization; add burst and failure margin, then verify with queue-age SLOs.
   Partition queues by region/residency, risk, workload profile, model/tool compatibility, and latency class—but avoid
    per-tenant queues unless isolation or volume justifies the operational cardinality.
   Use hierarchical distributed rate limiting with regional allocations and periodic reconciliation; no request should
    require a synchronous global lock. Keep a safety margin for clock/network skew and provider quota uncertainty.
   Autoscale on backlog arrival rate, oldest age, service time, active leases, and downstream permits. Do not scale
    workers when provider quota is the bottleneck.
   Enforce bounded fan-out, ready-node concurrency, delegation depth, speculative branches, context bytes, and
    retries per run. Admission reserves the aggregate worst permitted burst.
   Protect priority traffic with reserved capacity; cap each class so “urgent” cannot starve routine work indefinitely. Use
    aging to prevent starvation.
   For large tenants, assign dedicated queue partitions/provider accounts/pools when needed; otherwise use shared
    pools with tenant-aware quotas and noisy-neighbor telemetry.
   Cost caches, prompt caches, and response caches must include tenant, authorization,
    model/tool/prompt/schema/policy versions, and knowledge snapshot. Cache hits still emit usage/provenance events.

Implementation reference
Admission and capacity permit
PYTHON · implementation excerpt
 PYTHON

1         from __future__ import annotations
2
3         from dataclasses import dataclass
4
5
6         @dataclass(frozen=True)
7         class CapacityPermit:
8             tenant_id: str
9             workload_class: str
10            model_tokens: int
11            tool_calls: int
12            sandbox_seconds: int
13
14
15        def admit(requested: CapacityPermit, available: CapacityPermit) -> CapacityPermit:
16            if requested.tenant_id != available.tenant_id:
17                raise PermissionError("permit tenant mismatch")
18            if requested.workload_class != available.workload_class:
19                raise PermissionError("workload class mismatch")
20            if requested.model_tokens > available.model_tokens:
21                raise RuntimeError("insufficient model-token capacity")
22            if requested.tool_calls > available.tool_calls:
23                raise RuntimeError("insufficient tool-call capacity")
24            if requested.sandbox_seconds > available.sandbox_seconds:
25                raise RuntimeError("insufficient sandbox capacity")
26            return requested




Debugging playbook
287.      Decompose end-to-end p95 into queue, workflow, model, retrieval, tool, and human components.
288.      Measure turns, calls, tokens, retries, and escalations per verified success.
289.      Inspect tenant fairness, queue age, and safe-degradation decisions under load.

Production checklist
✓ The objective is cost and latency per verified success, not token price alone.
✓ Capacity is budgeted by tenant, risk, workload class, and scarce resource.
✓ Queues, backpressure, fairness, overload, and safe degradation are explicit.
✓ Caching keys include authorization, policy, version, and data-boundary dimensions.
✓ Every optimization is evaluated, canaried, reversible, and constrained by safety gates.

Unknown-effect runbook
299.      Stop dependent work and preserve cancellation intent.
300.      Load EffectRecord, intent hash, expected version, downstream request ID, and trace.

303.      Record evidence; accept, retry safely, compensate, or move to InterventionRequired.

Final assessment of the current framework landscape
  FRAMEWORKS SOLVE DIFFERENT LAYERS. AN AGENT SDK IS NOT AUTOMATICALLY A DURABLE W
  Frameworks solve different layers. An agent SDK is not automatically a durable workflow engine; a protocol is not an
  authorization system; a trace product is not an audit ledger; and a managed agent runtime does not remove the
  enterprise’s responsibility for data, policy, and outcome risk.

Overall industry direction
305.     From chains to explicit state: graph and workflow abstractions are replacing invisible prompt loops.
306.     From synchronous chat to sessions/tasks: managed runtimes and A2A now model asynchronous,
    interruptible, long-running work.
307.     From bespoke plugins to protocols: MCP standardizes tools/resources; A2A standardizes remote-agent
    interaction.
308.     From logs to trajectories: framework traces and OpenTelemetry conventions increasingly capture handoffs,
    tools, tokens, and evaluation signals.
309.     From single-vendor demos to gateways: enterprises need model, tool, policy, identity, and telemetry
    gateways to retain control across frameworks.
310.     From prompt safeguards to capability security: OWASP’s agentic focus reflects that the central risk is
    privileged action under manipulated context.
311.     From “autonomy” to bounded agency: the strongest public guidance recommends simple composable
    patterns, explicit workflows, and greater autonomy only where measurable value justifies it.

Practical selection rule
Choose the smallest harness that expresses the agent behavior, the strongest durable engine that matches the run’s
consequences, and enterprise-owned gateways for identity, policy, tools, models, telemetry, and artifacts. Do not force
one framework to own all three layers.
